The Fabian Society has raised its voice against ' he intention of the Independent Labour party to ab-tain from supporting either Moderate or Pro- essive at the coming county council election, if ‘lr. Bernard Shaw can be taken as representing the views of the society as a body. For the policy of the Independent Labour Party Mr. Tom Mann is in a great measure responsible, and he appears to be well seconded by Mr. Keir Hardie. I condemned • his course as suicidal,and equivalent to self efface- ment, when it was announced, and Mr. Shaw boldly says it may do a great deal of active mischief to lie forward policy of the council, on which it might inflict an irreparable blow. As the society c ,rries great weight in Socialistic circles, the warn- ing which it has thus given, and the energy and imiependence with which it throws itself into the 8 ruggles of the time, may have the effect of giving a new aspect to the results of the contest now being c irried on with great heat in every district in L nidon.

In Mr. Cecil Rhodes the German Emperor has met with a man quite as imperious in his own way ti himself, and as he is not trammelled with the responsibilities of European diplomacy he speaks his mind to a German interviewer vvith a bluntness that will astonish the Kaiser. The Emperor cove•,s Willficli Bay, which belongs to Cape Colony, and Mr. Rhoies has very flatly declared he will not get it. Mr. Rhodes' statement that Germany has , neither got the best part of Africa nor administers wisely what she has got will not be agreeable reading to the Imperial mind. 'I here could not be i a severer condemnation ot German colonial nrimin- -1 iatration than Mr. Rhodes has crushed into a few :.entences, and it will be as gall and wormwood to the Imperial dictator, who loves to make his personal influence felt in the lowest as well am in the highest affairs of State. it is migniticant, too, that the sole interview vtil jell Mr. Rhodes has granted since he landed has been given to the re- presentative of a German newspaper, as if he wet- ' corned the opportunity of letting the German 1 Government know that its part in South Africa is ' quite a subordinate one.

The Niagara Club, which was started for the pur- pose of providing Sunday afternoon skating at the artificial ice rink at the Niagara Hall for the world of fashion,has caught on, as the sang Americanism xpresres it. The movement took the form of a club, in order to avoil conflict with the law which in force relating to the opening of pieces of amusement on Sunday. The place was crowded with bkaters on Sunday. many 01 them being mem- bers of the nobility. Among the throng might have ben seen the cheery face of the Solicitor-General, ir Flank Lockwood, who, with his wife,disported him- self with great ease and grace on the frozen wale r., One gentleman, spoken of as an American million- aire, was performing on'eliates made of solid gold, with the exception of the thin edges of metal which came in contact with the ice, and which, of coure, were made of steel. By this time, no doubt, the Sunday Observance Society has had its attention directed to this new development of the Sunday question, and I shall be greaty surprised if the gality of tbe club's proceedings is not contested before long in a court of law.

ONE of the unexplained mysteries of life is how difficult it is sometimes to get into a comfortable position when you go to bed, and how unusual to find one that isn t comfortable when you have to get up. . To a lady who was importuning him to direct her son into a paying business, Lord Rothschild remarked: "Madam, any business is good, selling matches even is a splendid business, if you do enough of it." You can't have your penny and your cake; but, if you are sufficiently able and unscrupulous, you can have your penny and some one else's cake. The sunlit snow on Monday distinctly accentuated the spectacle presented by the obsequies of the late Lord Randolph Churchill, and Vie keen frost pre- vented the white shroud from degenerating into ioconvesient lubh. Even in grimy London the 8:iow was so fresh as to have lost none of its virgin vicity, and there were glintings of sunlight. At Woodbiock and Bladon the scene in the clear frosty air was one of great beauty and solemnity. The passage of the coffin and the mourners from Gros- venor-r:quare to Paddington was witnessed by large and synipathic crowds, and the service in West min- s er Abbey, never more impressive than on such oc- c shins, was attended by the most distinguished men co every party and err ed and calling. The late I.4>rd Rand olph Churchili's political opponents were t ere in almost as great numbers as his friends— indeed, per Imps, even more rep eFentatively, for I live tried in vai n to trace any mention of the Northcote or ,•:.clater-Booth family either at Mon- day's functions or in the events which led up to t hem. Possibly other connections,too, of" The Old Gang," being but human, might not unnaturally hold aloof.

Mr. and Mrs. Gladstone are to go from Cannes to B arritz, with Mr. Armitstead, on Thursday or Friday. On the day of their departure Mr. Herb&t Gladstone M.P.,returns to England from his visit to Lori Bendel with his father and mother. Mr. Glad- stone's visit to Cannes has been most unfortunate a. regards the weather. The "mistral" has been blowing three days a week, and the other days have been cold, snowy, and rainy. Yet, I hear by letter that Mr. Gladstone lain excellent health, and does not repine at weather that give i him good excuse to remain among his books in Lord Renders 1 library.

TIIF EXAMINER, SATURDAY FEBRUARY 2. 1895.

11 hen the Knprrss of Austria first married, says " Pearson's," it was thought highly improper fir Royalty to carry an umbrella. Consequently, whenever it threatened to rain, she was unable to have her accus- tomed walk. She moped so limn that her husband declared that thenceforth umbrehas should not lee con- trary to etiquette. Not very long ago the Duke of Norfolk, while walking throu 411 trie streets of London, happen‘ d to see an old lady in evident distress. She wanted a cab, and could not attract the cabman's notice. His Grace called a vehicle, and saw her into it safely. To his surprise he ; found three coppers slipped into his hand, and the old lady said, "There you are, my man ; go and get your- self a glass of beer!" A good many funny little incidents are connected with the Princess Louise's stay at Dornden. On an occasion an elderly country clergyman went to pay a call. He rang at the front-door bell, and the door was immediately opened by a man in shirt-sleeves. "The Princess is gone out driving," he said. ." Kindly give her this card,', said the old clergyman. Only later did he find out that the person he had taken for one of the servants was the Marquis of Lorne, who had been doing a bit of carpentering in the hall. Dr. Parkhurst, a celebrated American preacher, has , come-1 a new word, and its aptness is quite likely to ; make it famous. The word is" Andromania," and of it he says : " There is an element in the feminine world that lS sugering from what I shall venture to call ' Atidromania."fhe word is not an English one, for the reason, I suppose, that the English language-makers never supposed that we should need such a term. It is constructed on the same principle as the word' Anglo- mania,' which means a passionate aping of everything that is English. • Andromonia ' means, similarly, a passionate aping of everything that is mannish." Another time the Princess and her husband went to afternoon service at the church on Rustall Common, near Tunbridge Wells. They arrived rather early and were shown into a good seat. A few minutes alterwards two maiden ladies—regular attendants at the church, and ardent in all parish work—to whom the pew rightly belonged, arrived, and were surprised and rather indignant to find strangers in their places. They lost no time in informing them that they must move, and before the pew-opener could interfere the Princesa was out in the aisle. She only smiled good-naturedly and suggested that perhaps it would be better if she went into the free seats.

Curran, M.P., one of the members of the I Irish Party, tells the London correspondent of the i Sheffield Indepencent " an amusing story of Mr. Tim ; Healy. Mr. Healy's father-in-law is Mr. T. D. Sulli- van, whom everybody knows both as poet and politican. On the occasion of the marriage of Mr. Healy to Miss Sullivan, when Mr. Healey was leaving with his bride tor the honeymoon, in the flutter of the moment he picked up Mr. Sullivan's umbrella instead of his own from the hat rack. '* Excuse me, Tim," said Mr. Sulli- van, "I have five &ugh ters but only one umbrella ; you will leave me that ?

Health-Giving Perfumes. There seems to be, undoubtedly is, something posi- tively health and nourishment and strength giving in the influence of sweet and powerful perfumes on the nerves-3 subtle something yet unexplained. What we get of most value in the country, physicians and phdom- ophers say, are the sweet smells of grass and flowers .nd trees. It is not chiefly the spirits, the alco3ol,in cologne that invigorates; it is tho essence or aromatic oils. The delight that a rose gives to everybody is due to some mysterious stimulus the rose fragrance has for the nerves. Half the delight in walking in old lanes in the country comes from the invisible trailing steamers and floating clouds of flower incense in the air.

Soda in Tea. 1 Many housewives are accustomed to put in a small pinch of soda with the tea ; more especially is this the case in London, whera the water is so very hard. There is nothing injurious in such addition, though it would be much better to add the soda to the water in a suit- able vessel, stir well up, and let settle. Rain water col- lected in proper tanks and well filtered is not onl) much better for making tea with, but much more wholesome. The following is a good plan for making tea :—Pour into an earthenware teapot some boiling water ; empty it out, and while still het and steaming put in the tra. Add enough boiling soft water to wet thoroughly, and set it close to the fire to steam for six minutes. Then pour in the quantity of boiling water required from ill:. kettle, and it is ready for use.

"That Nasty Coagh !" Coughs are even more common than colds. A clugh is due to the irritation of a part of the air-passages or of the nerves au pp,ying them. In bronchitis, for example, the cough is due to the presence and accumulation of mucus, and is the effort of Nature to dislodge it. Very often, however—and these cases are too fregoently over- looked—a cough is due to local causes. The tonsils may be enlarged, or the uvula—that little tongue dependent between the tor.. Is— may be swollen and elongated, or there may I a small granulations on the back of the throat. In these cases local treatmect is necessary, and drugs are, for the most part, of little service. Cough may also depend upon a reflex irritation from the ear, (it is known that the bad habit of picking the ear with the head of a pin of ten induces a cough), or from a dis- ordered stoma ch (the morning cough of many pAople is closely dependent upon dissipation the previous ev, nine). The net vs may even be irritated directly by the presence 1 of a tumour pressing upon it. The cough, in these cases, has a distinct character—it is stridulous. THE DOUBLE LIFE: OR, THE HAMPTON MYSTERY By the Author of "DAUSWOOD."

CHAPTER LIII. I find thee apt; And duller shouldst thou be than the fat weed That roots itself in ease on Lethe wharf, Wouldst thou not stir in this.

HAMLET. Hamptonwold was one of those fair and stately homes which are the glory of the United Kingdom. The mansion was built in the castellated style, and presented an appearance at once imposing and magnificent. It was set like a gem in the midsfof lovely flower gardens and pleasure grounds. A noble wood was comprised in its wide domain, and here the fallow-deer ran wild, and pheasants and other winged game fluttered in undisturbed security. The lawn in front of the mansion, green and smooth and velvety, sloped gently down to meet the waters of the Bristol Channel. On the morning appointed for the secret meeting between the Lady Beatrice and those she so dearly loved, Lord Trevelyan was walking alone upon this lawn, his hands clasped behind him, hie head half drooping upon his Tbreast. There was a sor- rowfnl, anxious and lonely expression on his grand old face. His great gloomy eyes were full of a brooding sadness. He had been thinking of Giralda, and wondering if she had crossed his path and won his love only to disappear from his life , for-ever. In the midst of his gloom and despondency a brisk step was heard on the walk behind him, rapidly approaching, and turning hastily he heheld , Lord Adlow. At the first glance the old lord was struck with the marvellous change defeat and rage had wrought in his evil-minded nephew. All the airs and graces, all the dandyism of dress and affectations of !neuter, had dropped from Adlowe as a worn-out disguise. He looked dark and haggard, wild and , sinister. There was a vengeful glow on his face. His hair was dishevelled; his attire slouching and neglected. He had an air of utter recklessness, and the marquis, meeting his gaze, shrunk before. the burning glare of his red, dark-circled eyes. "What's the matter, Adlowe?" he exclaimed, In amazement. "I was at Pencoed, and hearing that you were here, came over to tell you the news," responded Adlowe, in his hollow voice. "I have found Giralda." Lord Trevelyan stalled joyfully. ' " Where is she?" he demanded. " You have brought her with you?" No, my lord. She was with Lord Grosvenor, and utterly refused to come. She told me to tell you that she would never forgive you for driving her forth the other night—never! She said that she had loved you, but that she now hated you. In regard to her father, she bade me say she defied you to do your worst " Lord Trevelyan's face flamed. "She sent me this message?" he ejaculated, his keen eyes seeming to pierce to Adlowe's soul. "Yes, and more " replied Adlowe, meeting his uncle's gaze fully. "She has scorned my love. She has refused me. She ranks you and me to- gether, and relying upon her prospect of soon mar- rying Lord Grosvenor, she is full of scorn and defiance. Like father, like daughter.'" "And she will never come back? You are sure she hates me ?" "Sure ? " cried Adlowe, with a hoarse laugh. "She told me so with a sincereity I could not doubt. You can see her again if you wish, my lord. You can plead to her for a morsel of affection. She will soon be here at Ha.mptonwold I " "To see me?" cried the marquis, eagerly. Adlowe sneered. "No. She is coming here secretly with Lord Grosvenor. Homptonwold is a show place, you know. Giralda is coming here as a simple toffrist, but in reality because she desires to meet her parents secretly in these grounds. Her father is in hiding in this part of the country. Would you like to hear them exult over the cleverness with which they have duped the old doting lord of Trevelyan? Would you like to look upon the face of your in- tended assassin again ? " The marquis clutched his hands. His cheeks burned redly. He looked at Adlowe as if fascinated, but did not speak. Adlowe's tone grew wilder. "My lord," he cried, and a savage light leaped to his eyes, "would you look again upon the face of him who tried to murder you at midnight in your sleep eighteen years ago? Would you look again into the blue eyes of the boy you loved —the man you have hated? Would you hear him question his daughter as to the state of your health, the probabilities of a long continuance of your useless life? Say—would you see Geoffrey Trevelyan?" All the evil passions of Lord Trevelyan's heart sprang up strong and eager in response to this ad- dress, like hounds slipped from the leash. Giralda and his love for her were forgotten. The hatred and vengefulness of years took posses- sion of his being, frightening away all softer emo- tions. "Yes," he cried, his features working convul- sively, his voice choking with rage. "Take me to him. Let me look upon the wretch. Oh, that I might give him up to justice in the very moment when he thinks himself secure l" Adlowe smiled like a demon. He had worked his uncle up to the point he had intended. "Then come with me," he cried. "We will watch for him—" He broke off abruptly, as Detective Rush came hurrying down the walk. Lord Trevelyan awaited the officer's coming with a sardonic smile. "My lord," said Rush, addressing himself to the marquis, in whose employ he now was, and bow- ing profoundly, the hope of reward shining in his eyes, "I am come to report a complete suc- cess. " Success ?" repeated the marquis, huskily. "I have to report the discovery of Miss Giralda kevelyan. She is now in these grounds, in com- pany with her little brother and Lord Grose venor. She has gone to the Persian summer- house." The marquis moved a few steps as if involun- tarily. "My lord," continued Rush, his face lightning up at last, "lie is there, too--Geoffrey Trevel- yan." The marquis said not a word, but his face glowed savagely and with exultant revenge. "He has just landed from a small boat—he and his elder son," said the detective. "I have two men on hand to arrest him. They are stationed near the summer-house. Is it your wish, my lord, that this intending assassin should be arrested? Or shall you let him go ?" A lightning gleam shot from the black and stormy eyes of Lord Trevelyan. "Let him go?" he ejaculated. "Not not I have found him at last. The hour of his punish- ment is come. Arrest him when I give the signal. Now lead on." The detective led the way, Lord Trevelyan and Adlowe following. They were crossing the garden when they en- countered Lord Hampton, who was on his way to join his guest. The old marquis smiled at his friend—a terrible, mirthless smile. " Rejoice with me, Hampton ! " he exclaimed. "Geoffrey is found at last. We are springing a mine on him. Come with me and witness the con- fusion and terror of the double-dyed traitor when we arrest him. He is on your grounds." His lordship thrust his arm into that of the be- wildered earl, and the two, with Adlowe, followed the guidance of the exultant detective. The Persian summer-house, which had been ap- pointed as the scene of the secret family meeting, stood on the brow of a knoll at one side of the lawn. It was screened from all observation in the direction of the mansion by a small grove of trees —an outer fringe, as it might be called, of the large wood. Its view seaward was unobstructed, and in a clear day the opposite shores of Devon and Somer- set might be seen from its portal. It was built in the form of a pagoda, and had a floor of parti.eoloured woods laid in mosaic. It was completely encircled by widows, which were provided with movable jalousie shutters and silk curtains, and it was furnished with a bamboo lounge, an easy-chair made of the same light wood carved hanging shelves, on which were books, and finally one or two choice pictures on the panels between the windows. This summer house was a favourite retreat of the Lady Beatrice. She was in the habit of spending hours here, during her visits to Hamptonwold, in the study of books, or observations of the bright waters of the channel, with their many gleaming sails and hurrying steamers. Detective Rush conducted the three noblemen by a circuitous route to the summit of the knoll, and, keeping in the shadow of the trees, led them to the very rear of the little building. The summer-I:ooze was just in the edge of the little grove. and the thick low-branch *meg

The clerk, however, had a notice of his own with reference to the baptism of in- fants to give out ; accordingly.at the close a the sermon i he rose and announced that—" All those who had children whom they wished to have baptised were to sena in their names at once to the clerk." The clergy- man, who was stone deaf, assumed, says "The World of Wit and Rumour," tor February, that the clerk was giving out the hymn-book notice, and immediately ruse and said :—" And I should like to say, for the benefit of those who haven't any.that they may be obtained in the Vday from 3 to 4 o'clock; the ordinary little o and special ones with red backs at icsetaryat anyis. each, , L. 41."

were an effectual screen, rendering the position ol 1 the four men quite secure from the scrutiny of I any hurrying passer, should one chance in that direction. They crept close, with loudly beating hearts, softly adjusted the shutters and peeped cautiously Into the interior of the little edifice. • It was occupied Lord Grosvenor stood on the threshold, his face beaming with welcome as he extended his hands /to two figures which seemed to have just ascended from the beach. . Lord Trevelyan bestowed a glance at the young lord and then he looked at Giralda. She was sitting on the bamboo lounge, her hat thrown off, her dusky hair rippling close to her little head, her lovely eyes glowing with bright expectancy, and a look half hopeful, half anxious on her exquisite face. How Lord Trevelyan's heart leaped up at sight of her! Sitting upon a hassock at her feet, his head against her knee, was little Fay, his blossom-like face as bright as his sister's, and without her half. anxious expression. The two figures which had ascended from the beach now appeared in the door-way, and the four watchers looked at them with varying emo- tions. As the reader knows, they were Geoffrey Tre- velyan and his elder son. The former still wore his disguise, and looked like an old man. But at sight of Giralda he drew off his spectacles, and moved toward her with a quickness an old man could not emulate One glance into his eyes was enough for Giralda. "Oh, papa! papa 1'• she cried, bounding for- ward to his embrace, and burying her head in his bosom. "Dear, dear papa 1 ' Her voice broke down in wild sobs. "That man her father?" whispered Lord Trevelyan increduously. "That man Geoffrey Trevelyan'? Adlowe I Rush! you are a pair of fools I Rush replied only by laying his hand warningly on that of his lordship, who became again watchful and silent. "Oh, papa I " said Giralda, brokenly, "it seems . so long since I left home I You know now why I ran away. You have forgiven me, father ? " "I have nothing to forgive, my generous daughter," returned the disguised fugitive, and Lord Trevelyan started in his concealment at the , sound of those full, rich tones. "You have , done nobly. You would have saved me if you ' could !" He kissed her and blessed her with all a father's pride and tenderness, and then released her, held ' out his arms to Fay. But the little fellow hung back, and began to

cry. "It is papa's voice," he said, "those are papa's eyes—but it is not papa." Trevelyan hesitated a moment, and then pulled off his white whig and beard, revealing his black- ened hair and artificially darkened complexion— features with which Fay was best acquainted. With a cry of joy, the boy flew to his arms. From his concealment, Lord Trevelyan's eyes fairly devoured the face of the father and his chil- dren. Rupert's spirited, noble countenance espe- cially attracted his attention, it reminding him of his own at the same age, "So that is Geoffrey Trevelyan?" whispered Lord Hampton to the marquis. "How he has changed! What is he doing here at Hampton- wold?" Adlowe motioned the earl to be silent. He was hoping that Geoffrey would give vent to his bitterness in denunciations of Lord Trevelyan. Judging his unfortunate cousin by himself, he had counted on such denunciation. But for the moment the father had only thought for his recovered children. "Where is mamma ?" asked Fay, in a transport of delight. "We only want her now. Oh oh! Here she comes 1" He leaped from his father's arms and ran to the door. And at the same moment appeared on the thres- hold the LE.dy Beatrice Hampton I Two of the four watchers were nearly paralyzed by her unexpected appearance. And even then they did not suspect the truth. Not until Geoffrey Trevelyan, beaming with ten- derness, led her to the lounge, calling her by the name of wife—not until the three young people clustered around her, showering caresses upon her and calling her mother—could the Marquis of Trevelyan or the Earl of Hampton realise the truth. The Lady Beatrice, unconscious of any hostile gaze, gathered her youngest-born to her bosom, and the look of rapt tenderness, the soft radiance of a rerfect wifely and maternal love, that transfigured her royal beauty, rendering it glorious, was a per- fect revelation to those watchers. Not cold and proud and haughty now, but tender and gracious and loving as the most timid and gentle of women, her manner warm and sunny, her dark eyes glowing with a perfect 'radiance, she might have sat for a picture of the Madonna. "Thai Beatrice! " thought Lord Hampton, stupefied and bewildered. "1 must be dream- big!" The amazement of Lord Trevelyan was no less stupendous. He stared with 1-urning eyes at the picture presented by the re-united family, and his breath came hot and quick, and fierce emotions battled in his breast. Years ago he had dreamed of a scene like thisth with Geoffrey's children about his knees—wi— Geoffrey's wife to refine and beautify his home. The tears came to his eyes now, as he saw how near that dream had come to realisation. He trembled, and grew pale. Lord Hampton was not less pale at the discovery of his daughter's cherished secret, and he also trembled. Adlowe looked at the two and smiled again his demon's smile. Then came the voice of the Lady Beatrice through the oppressive hush and stillness with the clearness and richness of a flute. "Paul," she said, addressing Lord Grosvenor, who was still standing in the doorway, "you will keep watch for us? My father and Lord Trevelyan are somewhere about the grounds. Still they will not be likely to come up to this lonely place. We I may feel quite safe." „ "Your father, mamma ?" asked Fay, in surprise, lifting his golden head from his mother's bosom. "Why, he must be my grandpapa!" The Lady Beatrice smiled faintly. The earl had never expected to hear that name applied to himself. It sounded so sweet and ten- der from Fay's lips that a sudden warmth began to pervade his heart, and his soul yearned over the Bale fellow. For the sake of those bright, lovely children, he felt that he could even forgive his daughter for her secrecy of years, and for her alliance with a man whose name was stained, and whom he hated. He stole a look at the marquis. Lord Trevelyan's face was flushed with an awful rage. The smiles and loving words exchanged between the unconscious inmates of the summer- house stabbed him like so many daggers. They could be happy when the life of the marquis was wrecked! Geoffrey could be adored even when his soul was stained with crime. This was justice And Giralda whom he had loved and who had defied him, looked as happy as if there were no Lord Trevelyan in existence. He gave the preconcerted signal with a hand that shook as with an ague, and then, with a cry like that of a furious wild beast, he darted around the summer-house, dashed Lord Grosvenor aside as if he had been an infant, and sprang into the presence of those whom he hated, like a flaming firebrand. ...-kdlowe and Lord Hampton followed close be- hind him. Rush and the policeman brought up the rear, and standing in the door-way, completely barred the way of egress. (To be continued,

AN INTERESTING INSURANCE CASE

An interesting insurance case sent down from the High Court was heard before his Honour Judge Shand at the Liverpool County Court on Monday. James Richards,of Barrow-in-Furness, claimed from the Refuge Assurance Association £lOO under a policy of insurance elf toted by his late wile on her own tile. The ComPanY disputed the claim on the ground that the woman bad uppressed material facts with regard to her condition of health. The insurance was effected in June, 1803, and she died in August of the same year. either from cancer or from a malignant tumour. Medical and other evidence was brought in support of the Company'a c ontention,but the jury returned a verdict for the plain- tiff for the amount claimed. Stay of elocution was asked for, and counsellor the Cempany submitted tbat t ne,verdict was a perverse one, and that, in spite of it, j udgment should be entered for the detendants. Honour said he did not say that the verdict wag One which he should have found, but that was not a reason for his disturbing it. Stay of execution for a fortnight was granted.

_ RICH PEOPLE AND SHABBY

CLOTH Ii G.

, "Only rich people," remarked the astute Mr. Csta' tnissioner Kerr the other day, "can afford to dress .; shabbily." This is, of course, true, but their doing 110 has its embarrassments; they are given "tips" intended. / for persons in a very interior position in life, and, writes—, Mr. James Payn in the "Illustrated London News." I ! have even known one person with ten thousand a Vier ' or so who was given twopence in the street as an object of charity. In some cases the habit of being ill-attired is as much the result of vanity as that of the choicest splendour. Diogenes made tbe same remark upon the gilt youth of Rhodes as on the sordid appearance of the . young Spartans 'This is pride." But some peoPle, have a natural objection to fine clothes. Peter the Great told William 111. that what struck him most favourablY during his residence here was "the simplicity of dress ia the richest nation of Europe." He himself abhorred splendour; one who lived at his Court for many years tells us how difficult it was to make him dress oP to his position. When, iii 1721, the Persian Ambas- sadors were presented to him, he sat on the throne in a coarse brown jacket till some attendant threw on him, amid shrieks of laughter from the Empress Catherine, a coat of blue gros de Nar"s embroidered with silver. -This identical gal ment wag made for his marriage, and 'still covers his effigy in waic in the Academy st Science in St. Petersburg. SoMe folks wear inferior articles from consciousness that their position will acquit them of doing so. I remember as boy admiring the splendid gold chain and seals worn bY tireneral B—, the Commandant ot the town in which !my family were staying. "My dear boy," no said, Witt; his frank and pleasant smile, "that is the advantage being a Commandant; they are Mosaic gold and vrorth fifteen bob, but no one will ever suppose it." I knew a great whist-player whose insuperable objtction tc„) war-paint"—that is, evening clothes—often prevented him from partaking of his favourite game. Be showed me once a pleasant little note he had received from se intending host: `• Come dressed as you please, my dent I fellow; when a man is a trump, what signifies about his suit !" The man who despises" war-paint" is,of courses a very superior irdividual to the man who cannot dine without it; but there is no doubt that carelessness ie attire is apt to degenerate into something much more serious. I have known a noble lord and Cabine! Minister come to his club with shoes down at heel an holes in his stockings, and a pocket-handkerchief hang: log out of his pocket which no self-respecting thief would look at twice.

QUARTERLY MEETING AT WARRING- TON. • •

lIIPORT.kNT PUBLIC MEETING.

On Saturday afternoon the quarterly meeting of the P.S.A. Federation was held in Wycliffe Congregational Church. About forty delegates from the different affili- ated societies were present, and the chair was occupied by Coun. A. Park,J.P., F. ft.G.S ,F.R.Fl.B..Ashton-und..r- Lyne, who was accompanied on the platform by Mr. J. o.Taylor (treasurer of the Federation), Mr. D,Prestwich (secretary), and the Rev. J. Tonga (Warrit.gten). The minutes of the last meeting and those of the Ea. ecutive Committee were read and confirmed. . . . .

... - The CitAiRmAN briefly remarked on the business for the afternoon and of the dying out of the " P.S.A. Monthly," and ef the difficulty they had had of keeping in touch with the Federation Societies. It was desirable that they should get to know each other more closely than they did.

===== 0002601_18950202_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
ELECTION OF EXECUTIVE,

The election of the Execative Committee was then pro. ceeded with. Mr. J. W.Dent,(Oldham) suggested that out of the 12 members of the past executive as there were in one or two cases two from one town, ope should withdraw in ord-r to let some other Society be repro- ' sented. They would then have a wider field, and no doubt the other Societies would be pleased to he represented. Mr. J. Dunkerley proposed to withdraw his seat in favour of Mr. Dent's proposition, but the Chairman and a majority of the meeting were opposed to it, and he at last con.ented to be re-elected. Mr. Dent then proposed that the executive be enlarged, but this was not possible until the annual meeting, at which the Chairman pro- mised the matter should be considered. The following gentlemen were then elected to form the Executive Com- mittee :—ft-v. F. Flibbert (Blackburn), Messrs. J. Dun- kerley (Ashton), J. Barlow (Droyleaden), T. Bell (Newton Heath), Dutton (Manchester, Zion Chapel), :Chandos Wilson (Witbington), Thomas Wallington (Warrington), Quarmby (Stalybridge), A. Rhodes (Uppermill), Coun; Iredale (Mayor of Moseley), J.-El. Knighton (Stockport), and Mr. John Shepherd (Rochdale).

===== 0002601_18950202_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
PROPOSED VISITATION COMMITTER.

Mr. CILINDOS WILSON moved a reSoliitiSon that the proposed visitation committee be remitted to the Eeecii- tive Committee to consider and report upon.—Mr.RHODES thought the matter might properly be dealt with that afternoon when so many suitable men were gathered together, and moved an amendment to that effect —Mr. %Imam said, if they made a committee that afternoon he thought they should first determine the lines of action of that committee. With regard to his resolu- tion he left the meeting to decide. Two members thought Mr. Wilson should retain his resolution and another member suggested that they appoint a com- mittee and leave the modus operandi to the Executive Committee.—The Climax/0i then said that the amend- ment was that they appoint 12 representatives out of .the delegates present to form a visitation committee. He put the amendment to the meeting and it was lost. The resolution was that the matter be referred to the Executive Committee to report upon at the next meet- ing. This was carried by a large majority. Miss GORDON (Ashton P.M.E.) then read an exceed- ingly interesting and practical paper on" The work of a P.S.A. visiting committee." She said the visitors should make themselves at home at the places they visited and give advice kindly. Several plans followed by the visiting committee at Ashton P.M.E. were put, forth in the paper, and also numerous valuable sug- gestions.—The paper, which was a long, one, led to some discussion on the value as well as the details of the visiting work connected with the P.S.A. and other kindre,t

A PROORRSSIYE P.S.A.

Mr. CHANDOS WILSON said that the Withington P.S.A was a society in a large residential district, where two years ago there were scarcely any working teen, and nothing of which to form a P.S.A. They started with a membership of thtee, and the second Sunday they had the officers of the Society and one member. (Laughter.) For a while their success was not very marked,but now they had e membership of 207. (Applause.) Their whole success was the result of active visitation. They went from house to house in the first instance, giving out circulars, and when their Society grew they found a necessity ef confining their methods to the circuit. Every Sunday when a man was absent,a "visitor" went to his residence to report upon the reason for his ab. sence. Snmetimes the cause was sickness, sometimes otherwise,but it was always as well to pay a visit to the , absentee. Visitation did a great deal towards extending Ithe power of the P.S.A. movemeat. (Hear, hear, and applauss.)—Mr.J. Shepherd (Rochdale) desired to thank Miss Gordon for her interesting paper, and he. had no doubt that if every society would work upon the lines of that paper it would be a great success. He looked upon the institution as a capital means of getting hold of young men who thought themselves ton big to go to , Sunday School.—The Chairman thought visitation was the most valuable work of the societies, and presented opportunities of great value.—Mr. J. W. Dent (Oldham) I held with those whohad spoken that there was no work which they had to do whieh was so urgent and impor- tant as the work of visitation. They had a number of men who were willing to help, but who were not all cut out exactly for the work of visitation, and he himself would welcome anything that would bring into touch more of the members who knew how to go to a home and bring in the Master. All felt that if the visitors could give their hearts and souls to the work it would be a great thing, and he thought that Miss Gordon's paper was worthy of being widely circulated.—Mr. Rhodes moved a vote of thanks to Miss Gordon, and stated that the true aim of every P.S.A. society was to lead men to Christ The hearty vote of thanks to Miss Gordon concluded the business.

===== 0002601_18950202_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
TEA TO THE DELEGATES.

After the business of the meeting was completed a tea was served to the delegates in the schoolroom, under the superintendence a Mrs. IL Atkinson, Mrs.F. Gleave., Mrs. George Jeffery, and Mrs. T. Wallington. Mr. W. H.Payton then entertained the members with hn admir- able organ • recital of half-an-hour'i duration in the church. His performance tended to show, the high powers of the organ: and was.much,appreciated.

===== 0002601_18950202_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
EVENING MEETING.

There was a good attendance at the meeting which was held in the evening in Wycliffe Murcia. The chair was occupied by the Rev. J. Yoni.m, and in the eudience was a large number of delegates from various P.S.A. societies in the Federation area. After devotional exercises, the chairman addressed the meeting. He ARO that he was pleased to occupy the position of chairman of that meeting. A chairman was not expected to say much, and he was glad of that, hut he would like to say one or two things. He felt honoured in having, received in Warrington, the quarterly confer- ence of the federation of P.S..A.Sociejes and kindred or- ganisations Cof Lancashire and the neighbontine counties. He was not able altogether to congratulate .the federa- tion upon its title, for it took one's breath away, (laugh- ter). Be supprned• however he they might gall it the P S.A. Federation for all practical purposes. He *as glad that the Federation had taken hold of P.S.A.Socie-, ties in such a large area which it sought to c-ver. There were now 39 socieles federated, of which five had con- siderably over 1 000 members. He thought he could fairly say that the P.S.A F,sier ition which had held it, conference in Wy-clifTe church that day covered a toler- ably wide area of ground. There were also connected' with the federation, three Pleitsant Monday Evenings for women, a movement which had his greatest sympathy. He wiShed they cauld Manage it in Warrington. He had been trying to inspire some of the ladies of his con- gregation an school, to take up this work.hot the ladies of Wycliffe church were ladies distirmisird for their modesty (laughter), and shrank from publie notine with the mistaken ides, that their talents were not sufficient. He had been endeavouring to after that, and he hoped before another year had passed, that they might have pleasant Monday evenings at Wycliffe Church. If they did, they would have to an to Ashton-under-Lyne for assistance. (Laughter.) Their friend Councillor Park who had taken the chair in the afternoon, had on one or two occasionis rendered valueble assistance, and he had also been Pleased with the admirable paper Miss Gordon had read. There were other evenings peas ant besides Sunday and Monday.and ther had iu the Fed=ra- tinn, three Pleasant Wednesday evening:, and if they went on much longer they would endeavour to make all the evenings of the week pleasant He was gla Ito tell ' them that their experiment of P.S. A.'s in that church had been distinctly encouraging. Not only had they gathered together a large membership, but they had , reason to believe that the meetings liad resulted in a large amount of blessing. They continually heard of one or another, whose lives had been uplifted, and whose char atter had been elevated. It was pleasant to be able to relate that they had got hold of a considerable number of young men who hail .rifted away from the Methodist churches in that town,and got away from their Sunday schools, and from th•ir ceme,reeatieus. and wire now re- turninsr, to their old associations in the Methodist churches. (Applause.) They at Wycliffe themselves were reaping some benefit from the P.S.A riey saw per- sons at the evening services whoa( they had never seen before. He honed other churches would nseist in the work, and thus carry it on still more successfully. Councillor PARE, of Aslitnn.under-Lrne, then gave a paper on "Character in public life." RP said that that was a question to his mind of very great importance to P.S.A. Societies. •rt, question was, how tar, as SA. socteties,coulii they elevate the tone of their public lit' ? How far could they secure the government of the town or city, in the hands of good men, knd be made to serve the interests of public righteoueness ?Societies had to face two dangers : One of being too relgious, or he mieht say,ton otherworldly,"and the other of being too political. The first was one to which P.S.A Societies , were not likely to succumb, hut he believed that a great number of men thought religion was one thing, and morality another, ant that gettme sived, m'an', chiefly, getting to heaven. He was thanki el that this spirit was not entirely characteristic of P.S A. addresses. On the other hand, they did not want the P.S.A. Societies to deal primarily with labour politics or even social prob- lems. They were not intended to set class against class, labour against capital, or party against party. (Bea",

hear.) They were to serve is a uniting, and not as a dividing force. (Applause.) CLAIM •AGAINST A WARRINGTON NEWSPAPER COMPANY.

A COIPOSITOR'S GRIEVANCE _ AND WORK.

_ At the Warrington Borough Police Court, on Monday morning, a claim was preferred against the Warrington "Observer" Publishing Co. by Walter Adamson, com- positor, for Us. lid, balance of wages alleged to be due. Mr. Arthur Browne appeared for the plaintiff,and Mr. El. Woods for the defendant company. Mr. Browne said that plaintiff had served his time at the" Guardian" Office as a compositor for five years and went to the "Observer" Office in September last for an increase of wage. For something like three months no complaint was made against him, and also during the whole time he was at the " Guardian." When _ _ .

Mr. C. R. _ Adamson, who was canvasser at the _ . . .

" Observer " left that Office, his nephew, the plaintiff, also received 14 days' notice. He went to Mr. Gill for a reason, but could no.; get one. After receiving his notice Adamson was set to" feed " a ms chine,and know- ing nothing about it, the papers were spoiled. Mr. Gill then came up and told him to get his wages and go. His (Mr. Browne's) client's wages were paid him up to the date of his discharge and they claimed the balance of I.ls. 11d., because he had done nothing wrong,as he had no right at the machine. He would ask the Bench to make an order for the amount claimed.—Walter Adam- son,(l9)3, St. Mary's-st.,l,itchford,corroborated his soli- citor, and stated that he worked on until the Friday last week. His work was that of a compositer, and he knew nothing about machining, but was called to the news- machine on Friday, the 25th, by Weir, the machine- man. When he spoiled the pavers Mr. Gill accused him of doing it on purpose, and told him to put his coat on and go home. Cross-examined by Mr. Woods, plaintiff admitted that he had fed machines several times at the " Observer" Office. He received his notice on 29th December. _ .

. . Mr. Woods stated that on the morning following plaintiff's notice he was sent to print some bill heads, and a number of them were spoiled. On the Wednesday he was sent to print some " contents bills." and these were smeared and stuck together through his having allowed too much ink to be put on. There were 30 altogether. Mr. Gill complained to him about using a " four-em" to plane a bill instead of the proper "planer.' In consequence of his spoiling the papers on Friday the despatch t*ain was lost. He (Mr. Woo Is) thought the bench would be satisfied that ih, complain- ant brought the case to extort the amount claimed. For some time the defendant had been careless in his work and done considerable damage. lie argued that when plaintiff received notice he studied the way in which he could dam ige the company, and out of the 1,000 bill heads he spoiled 160 With regard to Adamson's state- ment that the machine was out of order when the news- papers were printed Mr. Woods stated that it worked all right before plaintiff went on, and also immediately after he was taken off.—Mr. William Gill (manager for the " Ob.erver " Company) supported Mr. Woods' statement, and further evidence for the defence was given by George Jones. St. Mary's-street, Latchflrd (foreman). and .1. Weir,9B, Hardy-street (machine-man). —Mr. A. Brown remarked that had lie known the cir- cumstances produced in the court he would sot have gone on with the case.—Atter a short deliberation the Bench dismissed the case.

SAD SUICIDE OF A KELSEY ENGINEER.

At the Liverpool City Coroner's Court, before Mr. E. Sampson, on Monday afternoon, an inquiry was held touching the death of Carl Thomas Blanch-Brain, aged twenty-nine, electrical engineer, and manager for a firm of electrical engineers, who lived at the Poplars, tielsby. It appeared from the evidence of deceased's wife, who seemed greatly affected, that her husband was a great student, and as a result of his excessive study she was of opinion that he had overtaxed his brain. He was taking medicine for pains in the head. He left home on Friday afternoon to go.to Liverpool en. business. He then seemed to be in good spirits. As witness did not receive the usual telegram from her husband she on Saturday morning wont to Liverpool and called at the branch works,but wit hmt success. She returned hornetand then found a telegram awaiting her containing the news of her husband's death. She went to the North-Western Hotel, and there saw the body of her husband. The other evidence showed that deceased was found lying on the bed at the hotel, fully dressed, with a pistol grasped in his right hand Close to the e'er, and life was quite evinct.- A policeman was called in, and he found a visiting card on the dressing table,which bore the words" lam married. For God's sake break it gently to my dear wife." The revolver was a new one, five-chambered, and with one out of three cart- ridges discharged. The officer also found a box of cartridges in the dressing-room drawer.—Dr. Pusey attributad death to injury to the base of the shull, caused by the revoiyer shot. Death had occurred some time before the body was discovered—probably twelve hours.—Dr. Clegg said he knew the deceased, and had prescribed U.,. him. He suffered greatly from the effects of ever•stuuy—a cendition when the nerve power , was very easily exhausted—associated with head ache, pains and general depression. He was accustomed to sit up working with a wet towel round his head. Wit- ness put a stop to it, hut he considered it had done him harm. Deceased was a very bright, intellig,ent man. but was easily exhausted.—The jury found a verdict of " Suicide whilst in a state of temporary insanity."

COMPLAINT AGAINST THE SHIP CANAL.

The Clerk read a letter which he had received from Mr. Smith Burgess with reference to the action of the Ship Canal Co., near Walton Arches. It ran as follows : ' Jan 7th, 1895, re road near Walton Arches. Dear Sir, —Some'hing should have been done to prevent the Ship Canal from interfering with the above. They have re- moved the hedge between the new and old railways,and have taken out the bank between the road and the river, so that the river is brought up to the edge of the road. Anyone slipping off the road, will have a fall of about nine f, et. Then about midway between the two arches they have fixed a large derrick, apparently for the pur- pose of swinging trucks of mud across the road. It will be a source of great danger to the persons using the road. I think our solicitor should see it, and take what steps he considers requisite for the safety of the travelling public, and the interest of the Highway Board. Yours, etc., Smith Burgess. —P.S. I have had many complaints from persons using the road,also from the repres-,ntative of one of the landowners, hence my reason for writing to you."—Mr. Linn ker said that he had forwarded the letter to the Shin Canal Co., and had received the following reply : " Jan. 9th, I have yours of yesterday's date addressed to Sir Leader Williams. I will be glad to know what you consider the exact posi- tion to be of the Daresbury Highway Board in relation to the above. So far as I am aware, no persons but ourselves incur any expenditure for the maintenance of the road, but as to this you will perhaps be good enough to inform me. In the meantime I may state (1), that I have a:ranged for a temporary fence to be erected at either side ot the road and, (2) that no trucks of mud will be swung across the road. A shoot will be carried over the road, but in such a manner as to allow of all passengers going without interference.—Yours etc., W. H. Hunter, engineer."—ln reply to this letter, Mr. Smith Burgess sent the following letter :—" January 11, Sir,—Mr. Hunter is wrong in claiming that the Ship Canal Company has repaired the road in question. The Company are liable for the repairing of the road from the swing bridge to the Warrington and Stockport rail- way bridge, and for the repairing of the canal bank ' from there to Morley stop (which said roads would be better for their attention now). The position of the Highway Board, I consider, is that of the Guardians o the road in question. We might indict the Company for damaging our road by taking up the protection tence from the side of it, and bringing a tidal river up against the road,which would,no doubt,be a source of jeopardy, and,further,l do not consider they have any right to put any bridge across the road without our consent.—Yours etc., Smith Burgess."—The Clerk said that he would further write the Company on the matter.

STRANGE CONDUCT OF A MEMBER OF THE BOARD.

The Clerk placed before the Board a cheque for £1.6 Ts. 7d for setts accompanied with the following letter from Mr. Abel of Runcorn. " Dear Sir, In reference to the enclosed copy of an account, I understood from Mr. Aaron that he had placed the orginal enpv before the Board. As it is an old account, and your Board will I presume, anon be altered, I should he glad if the matter were settled as soon as possible. The Clerk said that the hill could not be paid until the sanction of the Local Government Board had been obtained. —Mr. Swinton explained that he and Mr. Aaron (who is a member of the Board) were together sometime ago, and they saw some setts belonging to Mr. Abel. Mr. Aaron said that they would do very well for the Board's purpose, and he (Mr. Swinton) said that he would ask the Board's permission to buy them. He did so an the matter was adjourned, hut in the meantime, Mr. Aaron of his own responsibility had ordered the setts, and they had been placed upon the road.—Mr. Parr thought that it was unjustifiable and illegal for any member of the Board to act upon his own responsibility, and Mr. Swinton ought to have told them that the setts were laid down, when he applied for permission to buy them—Several other members also spoke upon the subj,.ct, the gen .ral opinion being that the hill should be left for Mr. Aaron and Mr. Swinton to settle between themselves.—Eventually. Mr. Darlingt,m moved, and Mr. Jones seconds I that as the Board would soon come to an end, it would be as well to obtain the sanction of the Local Government Board to pay the account.—This was passed unanimously, although several members did not support the motion very heartily.

WARRINGTON Y.M.C.A.

ANNUAL MEETING.

On Thursday,last week,the annual meeting of the Young Men's Christian Association. Friar's Green. was hold in the Lecture room of their premises. The chair was taken at 8 ,s'clnck by Mr. John Edrclough. J.P. After singing and prayer the general secreary (Mr. H. Stewart) sub mitted his

REPORT

which read as follows :—" In submitting the report for 1891.,, lam glad to be able to state that the. Association is sea dily increasing in its membership and interest. From the minute hook I find that the various clubs and classes for last winter closed in a very satisfactory manner, and an entry made in April states that Mr. W. H. Bailey, late hon-secretary, announced to the committee his intention of withdrawing from the post he had held for three years, owing to demands in business. As a result of this the interest slackened . somewhat amongst the members. Shortly afterwards a resolution was passed that a general secretary should be employed full time for the work of the Association. For this purpose it was necessary to secure a certain sum towards the secretary's salary and steps were at once taken for this purpose. A meeting of the trustees having been called to consider the matter they finally gave their approval to the resolution. A meeting of members having also been called an address was given by Mr. Hind Smith, of Exeter Hall, London, encouraging the development of the Association las suggested by the committee. The necessary funds were secured. The secretary commenced his ' duties on the Ist of October, 1894, and since then there has been a great deal of work undertaken for the finan- cial position of the association and also efforts put forth to increase the interest generally, which lam glad to say have met with success. In closing this brief report I would like to add that our hope for successful work lies in co-operation. Where there is a band of willing workers to " take up arms " there is little to fear. By constant watchfulness and prayer our association would accomplish magnificent results and it is earnestly hoped that we may not be disappointed.—lt was moved by the chairman, seconded by Mr. Wylie that the secretary's report be approved and this was agreed to. Mr. S. B. STRINGER (treasurer) then made a statement of accounts which showed that the fivances were in a satisfactory condition with a small balance on the right side. The reports of the various clubs and classes were read. The Choral Society reported that they had a very handsome balance frcm their last con- cert, and the announcements from the Ambulance and Bible Classes were of a pleasing nature, and also from the tennis and cricket clubs. The P.S.A. meeting are improving still ; the shorthand class had six successes at the last examination, and the Shakespeare class is all in a fair way, and looking forward to a successful season.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT WARRINGTON.

Notwithstanding the depres,ion in trade, &c., Wycliffe Congregational Church has managed to " hold its own," its income having been no less than £1,033 for the year 1894. The membership ot the church has also made a respectable increase, while the P.S.A.. Y.P.S., and S.C.E. are all in a satisfactory condition. All these statements were made at the annual tea meeting held in the Wycliffe schoolroom on Thursday evening last week. Following the Rev. J. Yonge's report there was an entertainment in which vari- ous members of the congregation took part. Mr. H. M. Ashton,who has now earned quite a reputation, gave two readings, in which his capa- bility for " pointing " American humour was fully evidenced. His success was as pronounced as it was merited. Miss Bessie Hutton had come from Moore in order to treat the Wycliffe audience to a sample of her splendid talents for recitation. She gave two recitations, one being grave and the other gay, but nevertheless, both were equally appre- ciated by the large audience. It was unanimously allowed that Thursday night's meeting was tar and away the most successful ever held at Wycliffe.

The magistrates are continually finding fault with shopkeepers who hang their articles for sale outside their shop doors, and thus give an oppor- tunity for persons who are dishonestly inclined to take them without much difficulty. The cases of thefts from shop doors which are brought before the magistrates are fairly numerous, and it must be getting monotonous to thosegentlemen to keep on telling each shopkeeper that he ought to take care of his goods. The ratepayers have also a grievance in the matter, for the cost of maintenance of tire prisoners during the period of their incarceration is a heavy charge upon the rates. How would it be fi the shopkeepers who by negligence had given the thieves an opportunity of stealing, were saddled with the cost of the prisoners while they were in gaol ? If this course were adopted, there would, 1 feel sure, be a marked abatement of the number of thefts from shop doors.

There has been a great amount of interest taken all over the country, over the quarrel which has re- cently taken place between the Bishop of Chester, and the " Times over the Gothenburg system. Both sides evidently lost their temper over it, and some hard blows were struck on either side. The Rev. H. B. Workman, M.A., the temperance secre- tary of the Lirerpool Wi.sloyan district, who spoke at Bewse2,--roF.:I IVesleyan G,urch on Thursday evening,last week,evidently believes that the Bis- hop of Chester had right on his side. He says,that the " Thunderer "has been tricked over this matter as it has over other matters well-known. He makes the strong assertion that the writer of the "Times" articles was connected with the liquor interest,and therefore was not a disinterested party to make the inquiry into the Gothenburg system. Mr. Work- man did not support the Gothenburg system out- -1 right,but still he was in favour of it if he could get no better. He is in fact net in favour of heroic measures. He believes in getting what he can, and then like Oliver Twist, asking for more. This is a policy for which a good deal may be said as it is to be expected that in temperance reform as in most other things one cannot get everything one wants all at once. A spirit of reasonableness, Mr. Work- man asksfor, and it is this which will prevail.

Mr. Workman attacked one anomaly, which all temperance men will heartily agree, ought to be abolishee.. That is the right, of the Quarter Sessions to overrule the borough justices' decisions. Wherein county justices are superior to borough justices it would 1)3 difficult to say, and therefore they ought to be dq rived of this power of vetoing the deci- sions of the " lower " court. It would not be unjust to describe some of the Boards of Quarter Sessions, as Publicans' Protection Committees,as they appear to be totally oblivious to any wrong doing of the publican who appeals against the closing of his house,and grant the appeal no matter what evidence is brought forward.

The Christian Police Association seems to be gaining ground in Warrington—a matter which cannot but give satisfaction to all right-minded people. On ',Monday evening, last week, the first annual meeting was held in the Masonic Room, Bold-st., when there was a very good attendance. The polies force was also well represemed, though most of them were in plain clothes, a fact which led one of the speakers, not knowing, to remark on the email number present. From the report which the secre- tary, Miss Bessie Dut ton, read, it appeared that the movement is progressing, and that other members of the force—influenced by their companions— were joining. There was plenty of speaking at the meeting, which lasted a considerable time, but the speaker-in-chief was undoubtedly the Rev. T. J. Madden, of Liverpool. Mr. Madden is an Irishman, and when he commenced speaking it was plainly manifest that he had more than his share of Irish wit. The first half of his speech 'fairly sparkled with witticisms and fun, while the latter part showed that the speaker was, as well as being a humorist, also a man of sense and deep religious feeling. Taking it as a whole Mr. Madden's speech was an excellent exposition of "wisdom, wit, and pathos." Hopes that the ~,,,•sociiition would in- crease in strength and influence were expressed by all the speakers, and its r Mu() was fully instanced by.the remarks from personal experience of two police officers from Manchester.

The members of the Board of Guardians were favoured on 1V ednesday. last week, with a visit from Mr. Jenner Fust, one of the Local Government Board !inspectors. Mr. 'Fust made a number of criticisens upon the workhouse, which no doubt will receive due consideration. The portion of the lions% which requires the greatest improvement appears to be the infirmary. The proposal to erect a new infir- mary has been frequently discussed by the old Board, who always appeared to be afraid of tack- ling it. Perhaps the new Board will he more courageous in T the matter. The Guardians" have,' I notice, appointed a Ladies' Committee to assist them in their work of Supervising the work- house. This is 'undoubtedly a move in the right direction, especially as the committee is likely to prove a very efficient one. .

mrdiano

During the past month ?he Ship Canal has been phenomenally busy, vessels big and little,continu- all passing along. Much of the extra business is attributable to th 6 new lines which have started with the new year. The Clan and Anchor lines have been largely represented, their boats also be- ing by far the largest. It is quite a refre,hing change to see the canal so busy with large vessels ranging from 330 to 360 feet long, and each and every one presenting an imposing appearance. It is also satisfactory to know that, notwithstanding the contrary reports of the papers antagonistic to the canal, the vessels have made the pas=age with- out any mishap worthy of the unneccessaty atten- tion bestowed upon them by "enemies." I have al- so heard—whether true or not that—the Ship Canal shares have risen 60, in the market,so that, with the increased traffic and promises of more to come. the general outlook of the Canal Company is not by any means as dark as some would wish to paint it.

T E EX A ;MINE 11,

SATURDAY, FEBRUARY 2 1895.

TILE leaders of the Liberal Party Mr. Morley arid persistently refuse to display that Mr. Asquith. spirit of abject dejection which Unionist orators say they ought to be possessed of at the present time. In fact, there was a tone of cheery optimism in the speeches which Mr. MORLEy and Mr. AsQUITH delivered at Newcastle on Wednesday. Yet have we not been told on the high authority of Tory newspapers that both the IRISH SECRETARY and the HOME SECRETARY are so hopeless of tho results of the next elec- tion that neither of them intend to again con- test the seat for which he now sits ? So lately as the beginning of the present week it was very positively stated that Mr. ASQUITH had finally decided to abandon East Fife and to seek a "safe? seat in England, and as for Mr. MORLEY it has been an often repeated tale that he does not intend to give the electors of Newcastle the chance of rejecting him. Both these charitable fictions were ruthlessly disposed of on Wednes- day. Mr. ASQUITH will again contest East Fife against all corners, in spite of the"stupid and male- volent fictions" of Tory newspapers, and Mr. MoRLEY has unabated confidence in his con- tituents on the " coaly Tyne." Mr. MORLBY's connection with Newcastle as its representa- tive extends over about twelve years, and in that time he has fought six victorious contests, the last one being the most remarkable victory of all. With such a past as this it would indeed have been strangely at variance with Mr. MORLEY'S known characteristics had he shrunk from another contest, however bitterly and stren- uously it may be fought by his opponents, and his supporters now have hiseassurance that" nothing will prevent him from putting his heart and soul into the next fight as he put them into the six fights before." The speeches of the two ministers were, all through, of a vigorous aggressive type. Mr. ASQUITH keenly criticised the" beggarly array of halting and reactionary suggestion" which is to take the place of dprogramme for the Unionist party, and the divergences which he pointed out between the views of Mr. BALFOUR and Mr. Ciremaxitr,Arpr will, it may be expected, have awkward consequences if these two gentlemen ever get into official harness together. The Liberals have, as Mr. ASQUITH also pointed out, ever since 1892 been engaged, in the face of great obstruc- tions and difficulties, in giving effect to the Newcastle programme, and a further instal- ment of the same programme will be the work of the coming session. He expressed a hope that three measures—the Factory Bill the Coal Mines Bill and the Truck Bill—which would make the conditions of labour safer and more humane might be passed with the consent of all parties. We are afraid that, in view of past experiences, Mr. ASQUITH is taking too high an estimation of the public spirit of. his Parliamen- tary opponents. They have not hitherto in the course of the present Parliament shown any very strong desire to improve the condition of labour, and there is little reason to expect that their ardent wish to injure the Government will be restrained by any solicitude for either the Factory or the Mines Bill. Mr. MORLEY'S speech was mainly devoted to a consideration of the state of Ireland and the prospect of the coming Land Bill. As far as administration goes, Mr. MORLEY'S two or three

===== 0002601_18950202_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
GOOD TEMPLARY AT EA.ItLESTOWN.

years as Irish Secretary show a record of which he • may well be proud. During 1894 agrarian crime, which has always been the most disastrous form of crime in Ireland, sang to a lower level than it has been at since 1875 ; and, taking crime as a whole, including agrarian, the figures are less than they have been before since 1837,the year in which they were first taken. This is not a bad achievement for one of those " miserable and ridiculous creatures —a pLilosopher," as Mr. MORLEY laughingly de- signated himself. It is now said tkat the Opposi- tion have decided to make a great effort to oust the Government at the very commencement of the session, and there are some cogent reasons for believing that this is a correct forecast of their intentions. One .of these reasons is the fact that Mr. Morley has framed a Land Bill . which he will submit to the House of Corimons at the earliest opportunity. This Land Bill is calculated to cause a good deal of. embarrassment to the leaders of the Unionist Party. The Protestant farmers of Ulster are as strongly in favour of the Bill as their Catholic countrymenin other parts of Ireland, but the Toil landlords are dead against it. How are the Unionists leaders going to reconcile these contend- ing claims? If the Bill comes to a division in the House of Commons, Mr. MORLEY anticipates the no less than ninety-two or ninety-three per cent. of the representatives of Ireland will vote for it. That number will includestich militant anti-Home Rulers as Mr. T. W. RUSSELL and Sir THOMAS LEA, and this will be an awkward demonstration of the " rift in the lute 1 which is to make Irish Unionist music mute. The Ulster tenants are apparently thoroughly in earnest in their demand for this Laud Bill, and it is just possible that, when they see they are deprived of its benefits by the Tories in Parlia- ment, they may come to the conclusion that this interest lies in a political combination with the Nationalists with whom they have heretoforebeen k at variance. Under these *circumstances it i 8 , highly probable that the Opposition will endear.: • 101 our to upset the Government coach soon after the opening of the session, in order that the Land Bill A amongst other popular measures may never be reached.

AMONGST the many adminstra- P.oer Law Administration. tive efforts to improve the lot of the "common people" which have been made by the present Government the circular which has just been issued by the Local Government Board may be enumerated. There ie now a wide-spread feeling that the administration of the poor law ought to be relaxed in some directions, and Mr. SHAW-.LEFEVRB, the Presi- dent of the Local Government Board, has taken advantage of the recent changes in coneection with Boards of Guardians, to point out at least some of the ways in which these relaxations might be made. It is mentioned that whilst work- houses were originally provided chiefly for the re- lief of the able-bodied in distress, and their ad- ministration was intentionally deterrent, the sick, the aged, and the infirm now greatly predominate, and this has led, or should lead, to a change of the spirit in which relief is given. Boards of Guardians are encouraged to humanise their methods, if we may use the term. It is pointed out that old couples who were perforce separated under the old law upon entering a workhouse,maY now be allowed to spend their latter days in each other's company. The appointment of pauper inmates as assistant nurses in the hospital, which is sometimes done out. of a mistaken economy, is strongly discountenanced; while, on the other hand, the policy of electing ladies' visiting committees, which has, we are pleased to eat, been adopted by the Warrington and Runcorn Guardians is comraended. With regard to the children, the advice of of the Local Government Board is well worth sot" ing upon. The Board notes with satisfaction an increase in the practise of sending the children ) out for education to the elementary schools, suggests that wherever possible they should ale° ' be allowed to attend the Sunday schools of the , denomination to which they belong. In fact, the course that is advised, is, that the children who, through no fault of their own, have to rely upon public maintenance should be encouraged to nolc as far as possible with other children of their own age, at school and at play, and in order to further this end they should not be clothed in a way that will distinguish them from others. This is all gon(! scam 4 advice, and it is to be hoped that Boards 01 Guardians will act upon it.

POLICE CHANGE AT RUNCORN.

Sergeant Woolley, who has been stationed hi the Runcorn district for eight years, is to be removed to Astbury, near Congleton. lie is to be succeeded by Sergeant Brewster wits) will come from Ince, near Ellesmere Port. During the time Sergeant Woolley has been stationed at Runcorn he has most efficiently performed his duties, lie was a member of the Sr John Ambulance Association, in which he took an active interest. Ile has been in ill-health for some time and his medical adviser re- comended a change, and to this request assent has been given. SHOP ASSISTANTS BALL A.T RUNCORN. There were about sixty couples present at the third annual shop assistants ball held in the Public Hall, Runcorn, on Wednesday evening. The hall had been lavishly decorated for the occasion by Messrs. Kelly, Wilkins and Co., of Liverpool. Mr. Ravenscroft's band was in attendance, and gave every satisfaction. Dancing was kept up with zest until the early hours of Thursday morning. Refreshments were supplied by Mr. Dutton, of Wilson's Hotel, Runcorn. _ .

- PROPOSED AGED PRnsoNs TREAT AT RUNCORN.

The adjourned meeting tor the consideration of the feasibility of holding this treat this year, was held at the Town Hall, Runcorn, on Monday even- ing. Shaw, J. Robinson, S. Coventry, and J. D. Jones and W. Millington (secretaries). The Chairman gave the report of the proceedings of t'm sub-committee, and stated that a sufficient sum would be forthcom- ing without calling upon the general public for the treat to be given this year. The report was con- sidered generally satisfactory, and it was resolved unanimously that a trest be given. The appoint- ment of the officials was then made, the secretaries being Messrs. J. D. Dr. Gastaldi, Mr. Posnett and Mr. Robinson, see the cab proprietors with the object of getting the free use of cabs. It was resolved thst a letter of sympathy be sent to Captain Le Couteur on his double bereavement by the death of his wife and son.-- The treat will take place on Tuesday, February I9th.

RUNCOItN AND DISTRICT TRACIIRRS' ASSOCIATION.

A general meeting of this association which is affiliated with the National Union of Teachers was held in the Conservative Club, Widnes on Friday. Mr. J. F. Moulton. vice-president of the association, occupied the chair. The executive's draft scheme for further financial aid to schools was considered, but no definite decision was come to. The follow- ing resolutions were passed unanimously: " That in the opinion of this meeting the new require- ments by the Education Department in respect of object lessons and suitable occupations, Swedish or other drill, or suitable phyical exercises, and a class subject additional to needlework are largely impracticable, 8o long as there is no reduction of unnecessary items in other parts of the obligatory curriculum, and no provision of further pecuniary means," and " that in the opinion of this meeting the grants for drawing in public elementary schools at the rate of is, Is. 61., and 2,‘. per scholar or per boy, in average attendance are inadequate in very many cases when compar?cl with the cost of the necessary materials and appliances, and should be incretgot" When the business was concluded a successful soiree was held at which there were about seventy present. Mr. William Randley delivered a lecture at Run- corn Free Library on Friday evening on "James Brindley, the engineer of the Duke's Canal." There was a good attendance. Mr. F. Wiewall, C.E • pre- sided. At the outset of his lecture Mr. Handley spnke of the intellectual pleasure and profit to be derived from a study of biography in which they had to do with the lives of those who had taken an important part in the shaping of the nation's his- tory, and he referred to a series of biographical works written by Dr. Smiles which were in the Library, and which Mr. Jones would only be too pleased to lend to inquirers. He then alluded at some length to the career of Brindley and his con- nection with the Duke of Bridgewater. He in- cidentally mentioned that the Bridgewater Canal took five year to construct, and cost £220,000. A cordial vote of thanks was passed to Mr. Mend- ley, on the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. Gowerlock. _

During au interval Mr. Imison, speaking on behalf of the relief committee and the public generally,moved a cordial vote of thanks to the Negro Nomads. The state of things in Run- corn was, he said, quite exceptional. They could only hove that the commercial gloom would pass away. awl the stocks which had accumulated would soon meet with purchasers, and that work woiild be resumed. They were being liberally as- ; sisted by townsmen,but when strangers gave them their awsiQtance they deserved a double vote of thanks.—Mr. .1. W. Lightburn seconded, and said there was no class more deserving of sympathy than the studl shopkeepers who were compelled at times like the present to put so much in their ledgers which they might ne‘er Fret back again.—A stump sip-4Th was afterwards delivered by Mr. Holliday (Professor snowball), et the conclusion of which he acknowledged the vote of thanks. RUNCORN POLICE STATION.

The sub-committee of the Cheshire Standing Joint Committee who recently inspected Runcorn Police Station reported to the meeting at Chester on Saturday, that they had considered the following extract ft llt Maj,sty's Inspector's teport "The cells and offices at Ruucorn are ill-adapted for so important a place, and a new station is certainly necessary." They had also before them a notice from the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners re- quiring something to be ilone at the inspector's quarters without delay. Upon an inspection of the cells they found that Om were certainly dark but otherwise in a Sanitary condition. The outside offices in the inspector's quarters were in an insani- tary condition. The kitchen was dark and in that sense unsatisfactory. The sub-committee did not consider that a new station was required, but that improvements should be made to the inspector's quarters and other domestic offices, and that the cells should be removed. Thee could not be pro- perly carried out upon the property of the county so that it would be necessary to acquire some ad-' joining property for the purpose. Adjoining the station was a plot of land with some old stables on it, belonging to Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Company, and which, if acquired, would in their opinion,permit of improvement being carried out which would place the station in a thoroughly satisfactory condition, and at the same time pro- vide better and more convenient accommodation for the magistrates in the shape of a new retiring room, the present one being dark and uncomfort- able. They therefore recommended that the plot , of land should he acquired, if possible, so that the suggested alterations and improvements might be carried out. They also recommended that they should be re-appointed with power to negotiate the, purchase of the land referred to from Messrs Greenall, Whitley and Co. and be authorised to in- struct the county surveyor to prepare plans and estimates for the alterations and improvements before indicated, repotting thereon to a future meeting of the Committee, and in the meantime to authorise the county surveyor, if it was necessary to do so, to carry out the sanitary work referred to in the notice received from the sanitary inspector. —The Committee was re-appointed and ;authorised to enter into negotiations with Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co. as recommended. LOYAL WILLIAM THE FOURTH LODGE, RUNCORN.

DEATH' OF MRS. WlincuNTE.

There were general expressions ot regret in St. Helen's on Tuesday vvhen it becamo known that Mrs. Wliectinie, wife of Alderman D. liPliechnie, and ex-Mayor of the borough, had died early that morning at Bournemouth. Mrs. M'Kecbuie had not enjoyed good health for a considerable time past, and early in December the family left their residence, RoclestOn Grange, St. Anne's. for Bourne- mouth, in the hope that the change of climate for the winter months would have a beneficial effect. These hopes, however, have not, been realised. The members of the family are well known in Runcorn, Mrs. A. R. Norman, of higher Runcorn, being a daughter, and many residents of this district sym- pathise with them in their bereavement.
