SHOCKING CRUELTY TO A HOESE AT WIDNES,

_ At the Widnes Petty Sessions on Thursday, before Councillor W. W. Gossage (in the chair), the Mayor (Alderman S. Sadler), and Mr. J. Beecham. Andrew llogarty, coal dealer, Moss Bank-road, was sum- moned for cruelly ill-treating a horse. The defen- dant, on the 3rd ult., was seen by Police-sergeants fhwaites and Carter unmercifully beating and kicking the animal in Lugsdale-road.—lnspector Luff, of Warrington, said it was the worst case he had ever seen. He saw the horse on Thursday. The horse was apparently being starved to death, and its bones almost protruded through its skin- Ile believed the horse had since been slaughtered.— The Chairman said he lonsidered it the worst case of the kind which had been before the court, and the defendant must pay 40. i. and costs,or go to gaol for a month HEARTLESS CASE OF WIFE DESERTIO:q AT WIDNES.

On Thursday. at Widnes Petty Sessions, before Councillor W. W. Gossage (in the chair), the Mayor (Alderman S. Sadler), and Mr. J. Beecham, a labourer named John Machin, of no fixed abode,was brought up on a warrant charged with deserting his wife and child, who hereby became chargeable to the common fund of the Prescot Union, on the 2nd of May last. Mr. A. Wood, relieving officer, said the case was a utast cruel one. The prisoner left his wife on the 25th of March last,the day upon she was confined. They were living at the time at 13, Fraser's-court. St. Helens, and the prisoner was in work, earning 4s. a day. The wife lived upon the charity of neighbours for nine days after her husband left her, and then went to Widnes, and lived with some friends there. The prisoner had been wandering about the country drinking, but on Saturday last his wife saw him passing the work- house. The cost to the guardians had been .£l6. The prisoner, in defence, said he left his wife to go in search of work.—He was committed to gaol for two months with hard labour.

HALTuN PARISH COUNCIL MEETINU.

===== 0002601_18950202_art0069.txt | included=true | included =====
GEPTING TO BUSINESS.

On Tuesday evening, the second meeting of this coun- cil was held in the Girls' Schoolroom, Halton. The Rev. G. D. Wray occupied the chair, and there were also present: Messrs. W. McCullock, .7. S. Jeacock, R. Hughes, J. Looker, S. Barrow, J. Astbury, J. Ackerley, H. Reynolds. J.P., C. Birch, and A. Woodier (secre- tary). Mr. MeCullock thought it would be advisable for tee council, before proceeding to the business on the agenda paper, to take into consideration the question af providing relief for the unemployed in the parish:—The council agreed to consider the matter.—Mr. lacCullock proposed that a committee be formed.--Mr. Hughes seconded, Mr. Reynolds and • Mr. Looker supported. This was carried unanimously.—A Committee consisting of the following gentlemen WAS appointed: The Chair- man, and Messrs. Reynolds, Faulkner, Wright, Hughes and Looker.—lt was slated that no particulars had been sent in of the expenses of the recent election in the parish, but it was decided to 'stele a precept on the overseers for £2s.—After some discussion it Was agreed to, that the payment for the use of the schoolroom , for the holding of the parish meetings should be 2s. id. per meeting in winter, and is. in summer.— Mr. Wright, the [Talton representative on the Run- corn Rural District Council, attended the meeting to make a statement regarding the suggested formation of a Parochial Committee for sanitary purposes. A long discussion on the suldeet took place, the speakers against the proposals dwelling on the cost of the Clerk's salary. It was ultimately agreed to form a parochial committee to consist of the members of the council.—Mr.Jeacock introduced the question of allot- ments, but nothing definitely was decided in the matter, it having again to be considered at a future meeting if the council.—The Rev. G. D. Wray moved that a corn- mittee be appointed for considering the question ot Halton Charities, particulars of which charities will be found in another column. He said that all the Halton ! charities, with the exception of the St. Thomas's day charity, came under the head of Ecclesiastical charities, and could not be touched by the Council. But previ- ously the charities had not been dispensed as the donors had indicated. Their had been given to all in want, Irrespective of the religious denomination they belonged, whereas they were intended for those belenging to the Church of England. No question had been asked to what creed or sect the recipients belonged. But whether they would kave to confine it in the future to those to whom it was left, remained to be seen. A committee was appointed'; .and it was also decided to apply to the Charity Commissioners for their opinions on the charities. Mr. Stoke's had given notice that steps be taken to repair the footpaths leading from Halton Brook to Halton and from the village to the girls' school. Mr. Stokes said the footpaths in Weston had been re- paired by private individuals, but he had been unable to ascertain who were responsible fGr them, and he thought it would he better for a committee to be ap- pointed to investigate the matter. A committee was accordingly appointe4l.—This concluded the business.

RUNCORN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIEIT.

ANNUAL TEA PARTY.

In connection with the Runcorn Co-operative, In- dustrial, and Frovident Society, Limited, the annual tea party and entertainment took place in the Co-operative Hall, Church-street, Roncorn. on Wednesday ni7, ht. About 800 persons sat down to tea, the tables being pre- sided over by Mesdames Upton, Aston, Nixon, Cousins, maple, Dykes, Fitton.W.Cropper.Worthington,Pritehard, T. Bowyer, Rogers, Elliot, and Hill ; and Misses Cousins, Midge, Hough, Rhoden,Gleave,Nicholls, Holt, Worthing- ton, and Upton. The after-proceeding; were presided over by Mr. Sayle. An admirable programme was gone through. The Runcorn Volunteer Band, under the conductorship:of Mr. Outhwaite rendered several selec- tions, which were greatly appreciated by the audience. Mr. H. Montgomery (Manchester) was several times en- cored for his humorons contributions. Songs were given by Mrs. Temperley, and Mrs. Leathwood ; Messrs. Leathwood, Worrahl, Holt, and Burrows, and their singing was much enjoyed. The accompanist was Mr. Birkett Musgrove. The Chairman was thanked for presiding, on the motion of Mr. Holt, seconded by Mr. Royle. The report is signed on behalf of the committee by Messrs. John Holt, E I win Aston, Daniel Worthing- ton, and William Upton (secretary).

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT

RIINCOItN

BY 01:7R RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

The winterly weather if long continued, will, it is feared, prevent the working of traffic on the canals. At present the Bridgewater Canal at Runcorn ia full of floes of ice, and the passage has been kept open witta difficulty. Should there be a continuance of frost, it. will become blocked, and the tying-up of the canal boats will very seriously affect Runcorn. The shipping trade is one of the most important in the town, and additional recruits to the already large rank, of the unemployed will be most seriously felt. Shopkeepers, e,pecially small shopkeepers, are already suffering from the distress in the town, and it is with difficulty that some of them are keep- ing their establishments open. The prospect in the chemical trade at Runcorn, Is. I hear, a little more hopeful this week. At one of the works shipments of bleaching powder have taken place, and a few men have been re-engaged,

It is pleasing to learn that tie aged people of Runcorn, are after all, to have their annual treat, which is to take place on luesday, February 19th. It was thought by many that it was inadvisable to appeal to the public for support, and doubts were entertained that the treat would have to be discon- tinued for this year, at least. But the sub-commit- tee which was appointed,reported en Monday even- ing that, with the balance in hand from last year's treat, sufficient money would be forthcoming without calling upon the general public. This wa4 highly satisfactory, and the aged people of the town will thus hays at leant one pleasant break in what might have been, in many cases, a monotonous period.

The Vicar of Halton (Rev. G. D. Wray), was per- fectly frankat the parish meeting on Tuesday even- ing, with regard to the charities of the township. He submitted a detailed statement of the various charities, and it would appear that people in need at Halton,at least, are not uncared for, there being no fewer than ten cnarities for their benefit. In- formation regarding charities is. as a rule, kept in the back-greund,and this leads to many bickerings which would often be avoided, were the facts fully known. But with the formation of Parish Coun- cils which are to deal with charities—other than ecclesiastical charities—the public will no doubt become better acquainted with them.

It is not often that a workman is fortunate enough to obtain £lOO damages for personal in- juries under the present Employers' Liability Act. Rut a titter named Thomas Houghton, of Runcorn, who was employed by the Manchester Ship Canal Company, was awarded that sum by a jury at the Runcorn County Court on Tuesday. The injuries were sustained by the falling of a large tone from a crane, the tall being caused by the crane coming in contact with a passing train. The evidence went to prove that no one was appointed to warn the signalman to prevent the train from coming along the line whilst the crane was in, action. As to the nature of the injuries and their effect upon the conatitution of the man who was 66 years of age, it will be seen from the evidence that there was a marked difference of opinion between the medical gentleman called by the plaintiff and the one called by the defendants, and Mr. Horridge counsel for the plaintiff, facetiously remarked the he had always found such the case in similar actions.

There can be no doubt, After the repQrt of the secretary of the Runcorn Liheral Club, which he submitted at the annual meeting on Wednesday, that the club is in a flourishing condition. The membership has increased, and the adverse balance owing on the club has been reduced. The amount still owing is a little over £350, hut this is n•it very Retiring when it is considered that the building, billiard tables, and furniture, have been purchased. The secretary expressed the wish that the educa- tion feature of the club shoulri be taken up with more vigour,ftnel it is hoped that those in authority will tAlte the hint. By doing bo they will make the club still more useful.

Thr wintry weather im thr S anth of France is said to he unexampled in severity. lite cnld " wave Spread across the Mediterraviean,and "almost the whole of tLe RegeLcy uf Tunis is under snow."

MUM

CLAIM AGAINST TRH MANCHESTER SHIP CANAL COMPANY.

A RUNCORN WORKMAN IS AWARDED £lOO DAMAGES.

At the Runcorn County Ceurt, on Tuesday, before his Honour Judge Wynne Ffoulkes and a jury, Thomas Houghton, Lowland-road, Runcorn, engine fitter, claimed £325 from the Manchester Ship Canal Company for injuries sustained at Runcorn on the 6th November. Mr. Horridge, barrister, Liverpool, instructed by Mr. C. H. Unsworth, Warrington, represented the plaintiff; and Mr. J. K. Bradhury,barrister,Manchester instructed by Mesars.Grundy, Kershaw and Co.,aypeared on behalf of the defendant company.• Mr. Horridge, in opening the case, said the claim was brought under the Employers' Liability Act which was passed for the purpose of giving remedies to workmen whilst in the employ of their masters. The facts were exceedingly simple. The plaintiff was 66 years of age, and for ten asrs .he had been in the employ of the Manchester Ship Canal Company as an engine fitter at 31s. per week. The foreman under whom he had to act was Mr. M ston, who on November 13th, the date of the accident, ordered him to go and fit a 20 ten weigh- bridge, which was being fitted into a portion of the Ship Canal works at the quay adjoining the waterway, by which ran the sidings of the London and North-Western Railway Company. When plaintiff got there a gang of labourers were set to fix the foundations. It was not unimportant to recollect that the crane in coming had to pass a signal box, which was in charge 6f a man in the employ of the defendants. On one line of rails was the weighbridge, on the other was the crane, and it was constructed that it did not clear the next line ef rails. The result ,was that about eleven o'clock, an engine was allowed hy the signalman to come down. It struck the back Portion of the crane, and fouled the other line. Tn jib was thrown round, and the large stone in the chain struck plaintiff and he was very severely injured. The jury would have to say whether the naschinery was defective in the position it occupied, and whether the Canal Company's servants were negligent in not p.-eventing the engine coming down or warning the plaintiff. They could under the Act give linuehton three years' wages, which would amount to 041.—Thomas Eleuehton, plaintiff, in the course of his evidence said that when his foreman told him that he wanted him to assis.t in the fixing of a weigh-bridge' witness asked him whether the ether fitter, John Burrows, was also going to which he replied "yes, after breakfast." The weigh-bridge and crane arrived at the place about ten o'clock. The bricksetters were the first to get to work and witness stood for some time watching them. The weigh-bridge was on one set :of rails. He saw an engine g ing towards the signal box, and the man in charge of the points must have seen it. The brickaettere had set one atone in position !in the machine pie. The second stone was lowered, hut it had to be re-hoisted as it was too high. Shortly a fter- wards he felt a great blow on the breast and afterwards on the back. FIP did not remember anything further about the accident. He had worked with the crane once previously, but on that occasien there was a man to look after their safety. He was attended by Dr. Mangan, who was still attending him. He had suffered a very great deal of pain in the chest, back, throat, and head. He had not been able to speak properly &HUI. It had cost him extra for stimulants.— In cross-examination witness stated that he had been a fitter for 50 years. He had never had any wages from the Ship Canal Compiny when he was sick. Witness was working as an assistant to JAn Burrows at the time of the accident. No-one was told to tell the signal- man when the crane was working. He did not know that the crane fouled the line when it was cross-way. When he worked with the crane before, Moston told him to get platelayers to assist and watch the crane, hut be did not tell him to do so en the morning of the accident. Mr. Joseph Davies, packetman, in the employ of the defendant Company, stated that on the morning follow- ing the accident, he was told by Moston to go down to the locks with John Burrows, to let the men know when an engine was coming. He was there for two days. In cress-examination witness said his orders were to work 1 to Burrow's instructions.

Michael Mulhearn said ha was a labourer in the employ ef the Canal Company. He saw the stone, which was tieing hoisted* strike Houghton and knock him down. The workmen picked Houghton up. Dr. Mangan stated thab he was called in to see the plaintiff on the 6th November. He fcund him suffering from contusion of the right side of the chest, left side of the head and neck, and sprain of the left shoulder, and contusion of the back. He was also suffering from shack. On the third day after the accident he examined the man's back more carefully and found he was suffer- ing from concussion of the spine. He was still suffering from the effects of the accident, and was unable to sit up long at a time. He could not give an *pinion whether he would recover or not, but considering the man's age, it was very doubtful. He estimated that his chi rge fril- attendance would he about £2O Mr. Bradhurr, on ta‘ha If of the defendants, said they all had sympathy with the plaintiff,vrho was badly hurt, pa,d who had been a faithful servant of the Company. The jira-, flowerer, must dismiss sympathetic feeling. Outside the law, the jury had no concern. The question ! was, thr legal liability of the defendants or otherwise. He denied that there was Any defect in the machinery, and Also contended that the crane, was in no wav negli- gently fixed. They were in no way guilty of negligence. The nnly.susrgeslinn given with regard to negligence wag, that the plaintiff alleged that en a previous occa' pion platelayers were engaged to look after them. It was it nreaonable to expect platelayers to be engaged for the object in question. It was the duty taf the men them- selves to see that the engines did not riga into the cranes. lie contended that the Company was not liable unless , the accident arose through the negligence of the superin- tendent, and there was ne superintendent engaged in , the matter. He further pointed out that it was no fault of the signalman, and asked the jury fearlessly to dis- charge their duty in that case, and if they were of opinion that there was no negligence on the part of the Ship Canal Company or their servants then their verdict should be in favour of the defendants.

Dr. MoDiiugall gave evidence as to making a medical examination of the plaintiff. Mr. Houghton told him that during the last tan years he had had tyre attacks of cold, and about twelve months ago, an attack of influenza, which ended in bronchitis. fle was at that time away from work three weeks or one moath. He was now suffering from bronchitis, and a leeble action of the heart, which accounted for his difficult breathing. On examining the plaintiff closely, he found no trace of injuries from the blows He was not suffering from concus non of the spine. His preseat feeble condition was due to natural causes, In warm weather there was every probability ef the plaintiff being able to follow his employment. William Moston, foreman, gave evidence as to the in- structions given to the men. The engines would pass the crane in whatever position it was is. It was a van that caught the crane and caused it to swing round. It was the divy of those using the crane to warn persons of an approaching train. The Crane had been used on ! the same lines previously and the plaintiff was then there. Witness had never warned them before or sent anyone ti inform the si2naltnan. It was the duty of the men to Innk after their own safety. John Burrows, machine fitter, denied that he WAS the foreman on the job. Moston gave them no instructions as to watching. By the Judge : Moston merely in- structed them to undertlke the job to see that the work was done. That was the practice of Mostoo during the five years witness had worked for the Company. William Powell said he was responsple for the sending out of hydraulic cranes. The one in question was of the usual type. The plaintiff asked for the crane ts be sent down for the work in question. His Honour having summed up, the jury fgave a ver- dict for plaintiff, assessing the damages at £lOO. Costs en the higher scale were allowed.

SIGNIFICANT MEETING MEETING OF NORTH OF IRELAND TENANT FARMERS.

A largely attended and boisterous tweeting of tenant farmers, similar to those which have recently been held in such numbers over the north of Ireland, took place at Antrim on Thursday evening ; Mr. Samuel Black, .T.P., a well-known Presbyterian presiding. The meet- ing consisted almost entirelyof Protestants and Union- ists. A series of resolutions, similar to those adopted at kindred meetings, had been prepared, printed in slips, and circulated prior to the commencement of the pro- ceedings. Those in charge of the meeting introduced another resolution declaring attachment to the Union isetween Great Britain and Irelind and full confidence in the Imperial PArliament to redress their grievances and the grievances of all classes of the Irish people. The meeting resented this, and the result was a seriee of lively scenes. The proceedings lasted upwards of three hours, and eventually the resolution regarding the Union was deleted, all the others passing almost unani- mously. These resolutions expressed warm approval of the recommendations of the majority of the Morley 3 Commission.

Garnett, and Dr. Smith, Louisa Gleave (15), 6, Dutton-St ,was charged with stealing from the person of Elizab, th Beckett, 3, Flinds-st., a purse containing 2s. Id. Mr Hunt, Chief Constable, stated that on Mon- day afternoon about four o'clock, the complainant was standing beside Mr. Greaves' pawnshop, In Church-st., intending to make some purchases. She had her purse in her hand and afterwards put it in her pocket. Hav- ing occasion to use it a moment later, she put her hand in her pocket and found that the purse was missing. Receiving information that defendant had been seen about the shop, complainant proceeded to 6, Dutton- Bt., and perceived defendant coming down an entry wit' the pune in her hand. Complainant took the purse from her, d ,endent saying that she found it in Church- at., and tt,ce roll away. tier father, however, brought her to the Police S` ation. When charged she said, " I would not have taken it only mother told me I was only a lodger." She had been up at the Police Station several times on strong suspicion.—Mr.Gleave, the girl's father, said she was beyond his control, and she net long ago stayed away from home a week.—The Bench ordered her to be sent to gaol for IA days, and afterwards for five years at a reformatory.

Mr. Allen, solicitor, repre- sent the defendant. The complainant stated that the defendant was her husband, she being ‘married to him in July last. On the 9'h November he borne home drunk and pushed her out of the house. lie then locked the front door and removed his furniture from the house by the hack entrance. She knocked at the door twice but could not gain admittance, and had to sleep ot herdaughter's house. On the following morning the key.wes fennd under the door, and she went to he

tam in Surrey-street and took out her furniture, after- wards giving the key tithe landlord. Her husband was employed by the town and earned 1.94. 6d. per week. She wrote to him asking for support towards her main- tenance, but be had never given her any since he left her in November.—Sarali West also gave evidence. For the defence Mr. Allen said it was a case where one said that she was put out of the house, and another said that she went out herself. He submitted that it was a clear case of desertion by the wife and that she left with- out any cause. The man was willing to again make a home for her, but he was not forced to maintain her when she took her furniture from the house and, left him. The magistrates thought the case had been proved and ordered the defendant to contribute 53. per week towards his wife's maintenance. It Was totally unfit for work. Mr. Dixon, veterinary surgeon, Warringtm, stated that he examined the horse on the 15th inst. It was then very lame, and unable to bear any pressure on the leg. Mr. Leach said there was very little lame- ness about the horse, but it had a peculiar gait. The magistrates dismissed Davies on payment of costs, and fined Leach £1 Iss. including costs. ARE HOSPITALS A FAILURE? INTERVIEW FRO I THE ESSEX TELEG RAPE[.

Irt consequence of instructions received to interview a lady who seems to have been a lamentalele instance of hospital failure, an " Essex Telegraph reporter called upon Miss Louisa Fenner, at Graea lane,Ardeigh, Essex- lass Fenner told the following story: "I am twenty- nine years old, and since the age of sixteen I have been an invalid. Thirteen years ago I became ill with disease of the spine, and for three months I was lying in the Royal Orthopedic flospital in London. Then I came home,g,radually getting worse,theugh all manner of treat- ment was tried upon me. I became a patient at the local hospital, and for seventeen months after I left that in- stitution I took medicine regularly without a break. The doctor at last gave me up. I was on the brink of the grave, and all my friends looked upon my case as hopeless. But you see," she added with a hearty laugh, "I am very much alive now, and, in fact, have never feit better."

_ " How, then, comes this wonderful change ?" "It happened in this way," said Miss Fenner's mother : " A short time ago we saw in the newspapers a report of the unexpected recovery of a young woman at Great Bentley through taking Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. The case of my daughter and the young woman at Great Bentley seemed so similar, that I asked Louisa to try the same Pills. She was that day, as she says, ca the brink of the 'grave, as pale as the sheets, and her hands cold as death. I was unable Ca leave her for a moment, for she herself did not hope to live the night through ; but a lady got a box of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for me. The improvement when she commenced to take them was truly wonderful. In four days the blood had come back to her—the change was quite apparent—and in eight days she seemed to be completely restored. I shall never forget the change hr.,ught about in those eight days. Friends who came to see my daughter were astounded. know," Mrs. Fenner continued, " that if people knew the benefit Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have con- ferred upon my daughter they would, say with me that they are indeed a blessing, and every- body should recognise what good they can do. She has an excellent appetite, and whereas she had been so pale, she has now got back tho colour she had when a girl. Another remarkable circumstance in my daughter's case is this: five years ago an attack of sciatica took away all feeling in the right sid..!. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills have also restored her in this respect, and all -slm-ss hag 11 ~,peared. Her recovery 19, in

short, a miracle." "And," the daughter added, "Dr. Williams' Pills restored me when all else failed. For not only have I been in the Hospitals and undergone treatment of all kinds, but I have been into Lincolnshire, and to Harwich and Felixstowe, with the object of regaining health and strength."

- Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People are not like other medicines, because they cure by directly enriching the blood and purifying it from disease, and thus strengthen the tissues of the body and give tone to the nerves. They cure in this way all such disesses as rheumatism, neuralgia, partial paralysis, locomotor ataxy, St. Vitus' dance, nervous headache, nervous prostration, and the tired 'feeling resulting therefrom, , diseases depending upon humours in the blood, such as scrofula, chronic erysipMas, &c. They restore pale and Fallow complexions to the glow of health, and are a specific for all the troubles peculiar to the female sex, while in the case of men they effect a radical cure in all cases arising from mental worry, overwork, or excesses of whatever nature. These Pills are not a purgative, and are absolutely safe, even for the :most delicate. This applies, how- ever, only to Dr. Williams' genuine Pink Pills ; substi- tutes and imitations are dangerous. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are only genhine in round wooden tubes, about two inches long, labelled with a circular containing Dr. Williams' directions for use, and secured in a wrapper of pink paper, printed in red ink. Purchasers should look for the full name, Dr. Williams'-Pink Pills for Pale People. Anything sold as such without this is fraudu- lent, and Dr. Williams'. Medicine Company will pay a reward for private information. The Pills are sold by nearly all chemists, or rintiy be had from Dr. Williams' Medicine Company, of 46, Holborne-viaduct, London, at 2s. 9d. a box, or six boxes for 13s. 9d.

APPALLING DISASTER AT SEA.

NEARLY 400 PERSONS DROWNED. A disaster of an appalling character occurred in the North Sea at an early hour on Wednesday. The Nord- deutscher Lloyd Company's mail steamer Elbe was run into by another steamer and sank in a few minutes, naming with her about 380 persons. The collision took place about five o'clock in the morning, when most of the passengers and crew were asleep. The night is reported to have been perfectly clear, but there was a heavy sea running. As there were many vessels about, the Elbe sent up rockets and sounded her whistle, hut the steamer that struck her apparently took no notice of these signals. The stem of the colliding steamer cut right through into the engine-room of the Elbe, and the water rushed in rapidly. Efforts were made by the sailors to launch the 'boats, but the statements as to the number actually latiriched,varY. Twenty persons, most of them members of the drew, escaped in one boat, and were nicked up, after floating for five hours, and landed at Lowestoft. The British steamer, Crathie, which ar- rived yesterday at Maasluis with her bows stove in, and which reported having ,beeri in collision with an un- known steamer at five o'clock, t.hat morning, is sup- posed to.be the one that sank he Elbe.

WARRINGTON RANGERS V. Ti, SS N.-W. Loco SEEDS.— This match waS played on the ground of the former on Saturday. The visitors started the ball, and the play was of a very even character. After abut half an hour's play Plinston caught hold and kicked well, and the Rangers' forwards following up smartly, Savage scored, Siddle converting from a somewhat difficult place. At half-time the homesters were leading by one goal to nil. In the second half the Rangers almost con- tinually pressed, Savage scoring early on an unimproved try. From some loose play Evans scored for the Rangers. Plinston scored a good try behind the posts, and Siddle improved. This seemed to rouse the visitors up a little, and from loose play Llewellyn scored under the vlsts for the Loco's, Forster kicking a good goal. Time was shortly afterwards called, leaving the humes- ters the winners by two goals two tries to one goal. ASSOC I A TION.

GRAND GY N ASTIC DISPLAY AT WA RRINGTON.

MR. KINSLEY'S BENEFIT.. , BONING CONTEST. , On Tuesday evening the Warriegton Gymnasium was crowded to excess when a grand gymnastic display and assault at arms was given by the members assisted by the two northern counties' champions, Mr. E. Stanley andiMr. H. Haslam, for the benefit of Mr. J. Kinsley, the popular and skilled gymnastic instructor. At the time of:opening, the Mayor (who was announced to pre- side) had not appeared, therefore Mr. T. Morris, F.G.S. (President) took the chair and apologised for the Mayor's temporary absence._ . The evening's performance was opened with the dumb-bell exercise by the members of the boys' and girls' classes, who accomplished the drill with most praiseworthy precision. Next came an ex- hibition on the rings by the male members, whose excel- lent display evinced the careful tvaining they had receiv- ed. The club exereise.proyed quite a treat to the audience who fully appreciatedithe skilful and regular motions of the ladies taking part. Parallel bars were then brought forward and the members of the ladies' class gave a clever exposition of the elementary bars exercise, fol- lowed by the men who displayed numerous feats of strength and dexterity, the formation of a pyramid of 6 gymnasts-2 on the bars and the others on their shoulders calling forth tremendous:applause. Mr. Fair Turner and Mr. Harry Ashton were now announced for the event of the evening. viz., a three round boxing contest. When the. contestants entered the room they were loudly cheered and it was seen that in their size there was nst much disparity though Turner had the advantage in weight. In the first round Turner corn-, menced well and got in one or two good blows, but Ashton equalized with a clever feint enabling him to return, Turner. however gave back with interest and a bout, of sharp striking terminated the round.—Turner led off in the face, following up well in heat 2, Ashton occasionally getting in a well planted blow. Matters then became more equal, both opponents showing them- selves skilful exponents of the" noble art."—The third and final heat was lively but not , of long duration as Ashton by a smart stroke caused Turner's nose to bleed and the latter thereupon retired. A tug of war—boy's v. girls—was then covnenced and after a short struggle the " weaker sex" enceneded in hauling their oppon- ents Over the line amid loud cheers.' The position of th teams was then reversed, for the boys in the I second round drceaethe' girls across the boundary, but in l the next round the ladies succeeded in winning without much difficulty and were very- loudly applauded. High jumping was the next itear,and in this four ladies were pitted against four Men, the former jumping from the spring board and the latter from the floor. Both parties went along easily until five feet was reached when the men dropped out, Fred Bennion and A. Dickens being the last in. The ladies continued until 5 feet. 51n. was reached, MiFs E. Daintith belng the only one capable of clearin, thie height, though M 1391 L. Allen ran her very close and Miss N.Mottram also made plucky attempts to keep on. Miss F. • Jones and Miss W. Jones now en- gaged in a club ditet,in which both kept perfeet time with the music thronehout,,the whole of an intricate exercise. Double horizontal bars were then rigged up, and the members of the men's class gave a cleverly executed performance, some of the feats being excep- tionally diffictilt and just as successful. Rope climbing by the ladies was an innovation, but they went up and , down the rones with sailor-like ability—a matter of sur- nrise as well as gtatificia`tioh to those who witnessed it. The combined classes terminated the programme with the vaulting horse' both ladies and boys going over with lightness and grace. though some of the latter cau-sd amusement hy a little unintentional scrambling. -In all the exhibitions taken part in by the men, Mr, Kingsley led the way,and was followed by the two cham" piens. Stanley and Haslam. The latter were plainly all- round men of the " firsttwater," and their feats of gymnastic excellence fully realised 'all expectations. Both are the proud possessors of numerons handsome medals. The following are the ladies and gentlemen included in the classes :—tidies' cla,ss : L. Allen, K. Brundrett, M. Rrierjev, M Briindrett, E. NEW COLONEL OF THE W A.aar NGTON, VOLUN TEERS. Major J. H. Ridgway has been gazatted Lieut.- Colonel of the Ist Vol. Battalion (Prince of Wales Volunteers) South Lancashire Regiment and will now take full command of the regiment. For some years Major Ridgway has manifested considerable interest in the welfare of our local Volunteers, and the honour which has been conferred upon him has been thoroughly earned. Lieut.-Col Ridgway has been connected with the corps for hbout 14 or 15 years. It may be interesting to note that the colonel's grandfather, Captain Clare, was one of the old" Bluehacks." Brighouse held an inquest on Tuesday, at the Police Station, on the body of James Carty, 4, Rylands Court, Foundry-street, who was found dead on Monday. Mary Smith, daughter of the deceased said that her father lived by himself at the above address, and she occasionally visited him. She gave him something to eat on Sunday evening, and did not again see him alive. On Monday evening, she knocked at the door, and receivei no answer, but she went away again, as her father often slept dur- ing the day. John Smith, husband of the last witness, said that his wife asked him to go to the house later on to see if her father had got up. He received no reply when he knocked at the door,and thinking something was the matter, he forced open the door, and found the deceased lying upon the floor. There was then no fire in the house. The jury returned a verdict of " Death from natural causes." SEWING MACHINES Rani £2 18s. 6d. Treadle £4 4s. with handsome vnlnut cover —I and W. DUTTON. 18 and 18 Sankey-et., Warrington Advt. Tinsley, John Atkinson, aged fifty, a collier, residing at Golborne, was charged with at- tempting to commit suicide on the previous day. Two men who were on the railway bridge near Golborne, saw Atkinson on the railway beneath pull off his coat and put his head on the metals. An engine was coming towards him at the time. The men shouted, and Atkinson raised his head and was untouched by the engine as it passed him. They took charge of him and handed him over to a con- 'table. When charged he said, "Yes, 'intended to do it." Prisoner was remanded to the Warrington ' County Seeminne, to be held on Wednesday next. SHWING MA,,friNS Hand PE I.Bs. 64. Warrington.—Adve LECTURE AT THE NIUSL'UIt. On Monday evening the first of !..van lectures on " Tanning" (under the auspices of the Warrirgton Technical Instruction Committee), was given by Mr. J. Gordon Parker, Ph. 0.. F.G.S.. of the Leather Inclu‘triesi Laboratory, Yorkshire (l lee. Leeds, in the Lecture Ilall at the Museum. There were about 120 persons present, mostly ernplevere and men en- gaged ire the tanning trade. Courmillor Roberts presided, and said the lectures were being given with the idea of classes on tanning being formed in the Technical School next year. The lecturer dealt with the structure of the hide. unheiring and the treatment in the lime yard. The hide should not he soaked in the first water for mere than an hour, It should then be put in a second water, where it could be left longer, and then , into a , third and fourth water until properly soaked. The hide should not be left in the first water too long. A great deal of weight was lost, in the FoAking ot the hides, and this was because practical men were iealens of ad- vice given them by scientific men. After describ- ing the fleshing process, he said that it was neces- sary for all lime to he got out, of the hides by soak- ing, as if it, was left in it would cause the leather to be of a dark miner. He thanked them all for thA patient hearing they had given him, and trusted that they would go away inspired to know more of the science of tanning. At the close Mr. Long spoke of the pleasure it had given him to listen to Mr. Parker. and congratulated the Technical Com- mittee upon the interest they were showing in the various trades. FATAL TRAP ACCIDENT AT WARRINGTON. Mr. S. Brighoeee, counts, coroner, held on inquest at the parade room of the Polies Station on Tues- day afternoon. on the body of Samuel Brittle, aged nine years, of 15 Edward-street, who died early on Saturday morning,hv being run over in Edgeworth- street on the previous day. Henry Collins, 27, Beatnont-street. stated that he foes the deceased going along with other boys down E teeworth-st. When nearing an entry, a vehicle loaded with manure came out, and deceased not seeing it ran into the horse's chest. The driver did not know what had happened and drove on. Witness polled ; the deceased away, nod called the attention of the ; driver to the aecident. In his opinion he did not think the driver was to blame—Thomas Trainer, one of the lads with the deceased at the time of the accident, said he believed one of the wheels went ovorr the deceased he was not kieked.—A youth named John Burke, the driver, of the cart, said he was net aware of the accident until hie attention was drawn to it by Mr. Collin. Ile had not previ- ously seen the boys. He was unaccustomed to horses —Mies Robert,nurse at the Infirmary, said the deceased suffered from a broken hip and from internal injuries. He .was unable to state how the accident arose.—They jury returned a verdict of " Accidental death," attaching no blame to the driver.

STREET ACCIDENT.

Whilst walking down Heath-road on Tuesday night, a man named William Gessley, residing at Halton, slipped and fractured his right leg, a little above the ankle. Dr. McKenzie was immediately sent for, and he attended to the injured limb. The man was then taken home in a cab. NEW MAGISTRATES. '

At Chester, on Saturday, an adjourned Sessions for the qualifying of magistrates was held. The Court was formed of Messrs. Duncan Graham (in the chair), J. Brundrit, and Phillip Speakman. There were 18 gentlemen who qualified, including Mr. E. Inaison, chairman of the Runcorn Urban District Council, and Mr. John Mclntyre, chairman of the Runcorn Rural District Council. SATURDAY NIGHT POPULAR CONCERTS. There was a large audience present at the con- cert at the Co-operative Hall, Sankey-street, on Saturday, at the usual penny concert. Mr. Joseph Monks, jun., occupied the chair. An excellent pro- gramme had been provided by Mr. James Mills, which was exeouted in a manner which left nothing to be desired. The artistes were the Glee Party, and friends from the Workingmen's Mission, Bank Quay. The first item was a quartette, " Little Bo Peep," by Mrs. Joseph Monks, Miss C. Ashley,Mr. T. Pownall, and Mrs. Hassall,the render- ing of which reflected great credit upon the party, and proved an excellent start to the evening's enjoyment. They also contributed with -equal success," Anchored," " Now the grass with dew is wet," and "The Village Blacksmith," on each occa- sion well earning the enthusiastic applause accorded. Mr. T. Pownall gave a capital rendering of the "Larboard Watch," and was warmly , applauded. Miss Clara Ashley was very happy in the rendering of her song, "To-morrow will do," and Mrs. Joseph Monks was heard to considerable advantage in her song "Daddy." and was warmly applauded. Mr. Edward Pownall sang "The Diver," Mr. Millington," The Stowaway." and Mr.S. Hassell, "My Sweetheart when a Boy," each re- ceiving hearty applause. The Chairman con- tributed a recitation, "The Lifeboat," and a " Humorous Speech." Mr. Fred Gleave was the humorist of the evening, contributing two songs. This was the firstlime this artist has appeared at I these concerts and he met with a flattering recep- tion. The Glee Party brought a most successful evening to a close by the quartette " Good Night." Mr. Fred Atkinson and Miss Gleave Nacre the accom- panists. Mr. S. B. Stringer said this was the first complete programme that had been provided and , given by any one outside themselves. He had,l therefore, great pleasure in proposing that a very hearty vote of thanks be given, first to Mr. Mills for the excellent programme, and also to Mr. Monks for so ably presiding. The motion was heartily agreed to.—The programme presented at Ileathside Infants' School on Saturday was varied and inter- esting, and gave great pleasure to those•who braved the bad weather to attend. The following singers gave their valuable services:—Miss A. Oxley,Messrs. Faushaw, Newell, and Dutton, while instrurnental items were given by Mr. llopki,s (clarionet), the Misses Jowett,and Mr. A.Pickton ; the latter shared with Mrs. Pigot the responsibility of accompanying the performers. Mr. John Clark occupied the chair, and spoke hopefully of the work these entertain- ments are intended to accomplish. 215.—Fifty•three Articles, Sheffield Cutlery and Plate, Knives, Forks and Spoons.—GARLAND, Irounlonger, Bridge- si reet.—Advt. ig AGED lEOPLIIB TREAT COEIMITTEr..

A meeting of the Old Mea and Women's Treat Committee took place at the George Inn.Sankey-at., on Friday evening,week to balance up the accounts for the late treat at the Drill Hall, when there were present—Alderman Sutton (in the chair),Alderman Wright (hen. secretary), Mr. Thomas Morris, F.G.S. (hon. treasurer), Councillors Alfred Woods and Jas. Smethurst, Messrs. George Pilling, Peter Chritnes, Harry Toft,and Arthur Sutton (assistant secretary). The receipts from the several collection books amounted to nearly £75, which is a great falling off from previous years. The accounts were pre- sented and passed. The Hon. Treasurer finds that the claim upon the funds in hand have been ex- ceedingly heavy this year, on account of having to entertain over 1,000 aged people. He is wishful the, anyone who has not already subscribed will forward to him a donation to help him to produce as favourable balance sheet as in previous years.—At the conclusion of the business meeting the members of the committee, with Messrs. H. W. Cook- son, John fiedgcock, T. J. Hulse, Alfred Jolley and B. Grimshaw, invited guests, par- rook l of an excellent dinner provided by Mr. Thomas rfalton. Secre- tary, the two new members of the committee (Messrs. Toft and Woods), who all suitably responded. A pleasant evening was spent, Several songs, recitations and caricatures were given by Messrs. George Pilling, Hulse, H. W. Cookson; and Councillor A. Woods. During the evening the toast of those who had collected the largest amount of subscriptions was submitted. these gentlemen— Messrs. H. Tat, Peter Cbrimes and Mired Jolley— responding. Mr. Thomas Morris casually mentioned the fact that a sum of .£45,000 from their works alone had been deducted from the wages list during

the past year, and ae money always made money, he was convinced that, the shoe would pinch some- one somewhere. Rowever, he wad delighted to say . .

that this year there was a prospect of a happier state of things for Warrington generally, and he hoped that at the end of the year the subscriptions to the old people's treat would increase by leaps and

bounds. • ALL HOUSEHOLD Lixxs hemmed and marked, free of nharYs —.l. and W DITYrow, Sankey-0, Warrineun,—Aivt. CHURCH SUNDAY SCHOOL TEACHERS' MEETING. The quar,terly meeting of the :Warrington and District Stinday School -Teachers' Association.was- held on Tuesday evening 'in the Holy Trinity Schools, ffiuseum-s., tie Rv. E. C. E. Carleton in the chair. The Chairman explained that Mr. Ag- new, of the Bank of England, Liverpool, who had promised toread a paper on "The Sunday School Teachers! Opportunities," was unable to be present • owing to a serious illness, but an excellent substi- tute had been found in the person of the Rev. F. B. Tyrer, of Everton, Who at a short notice would speak to them upon " Sunday Schools in their rela- tion to the Church."—The Rev. F. B. Tyrer, in the course of an interesting psper,remarked that,while not concerned about the future well:are of . the Church, he thought that the secularizing tendency of modern legislation and the sgi,f,ressive activity of scepticism and infidelity 'gave just ground, he would not say for alarm, but for a most careful examination of their position and of their mach's. inery for doing spiritual work. the'Sunday school should be a nursery to the Church, where the Mas- ter's influence was felt. The Sunday school shPuld be as unlike the day School as possible,and it should be both bright and cheerful. There should be a thoroughly efficient staff of teachers. He did not believe in theological tests as applied to Sunday school teachers, who, however, should be, loyal to the church to which they belonged. Any teacher who differed widely,in evupathy end feeling with his parish clergymen had only one course open to him, and that was to seek another sphere in which to work 'harmoniously and cordially, or else to re- tire quietly from the rank of an active teacher, although he should not act hastily. The scholar should have the greatest amount of information given to him on a given subject, and the teacher should get from him the greatest amount in return! by way of answer. Individuality was the gift of nod, and to counterfeit that of another was to court conspicuous failure. Story reading and tales were counterfeit, and equally certain not to suc- ceed. The Catechism should be taught fully ad carefully, and the children's services in the school should be made as much like those in the church as possible. 'The children should form the choir collect the offertory, and do all that was possible as the. adults did in the ordinary services. A diEcussion of an interesting character followed,

Vl7 YCLIFP' CONGR EGA.TIO SAL CHU R,Oll. ANNUAL TEA MEEFING. A SATISFACTORY REPORT. On Thunday evening, last week, the annual tea meeting in connection with the above church was held in the schoolroom, Bewsev St., Warrit gton, when about 260 persons partook of an excellent repast. The ladies presiding at the varicus tables were :—Mrs. John Yong°, Mrs. Wylie, Mrs. Z Armitage, Mrs. T. Hawthorne, Mrs. H. Kith- bone,Mrs. Wm. Roberts, Mrs. George Brown, Mrs. Geo. Jeffery, Mrs. Harbourne, Mrs. J. Podmore, Miss Gray, Mrs. Atkinson, Mrs. Bowker, and Miss Beswick. The room was very prettily decorated with fairy lamps, fans, banners, and bueting, the following ladies being res- ponsible :—Miss Paul, Mrs. Gleave, Miss Miss Knowles,Miss LiZZie Knowles, and the Misses Smith, Mr. ' James Brown,Mr. Joseph Owen, and Mr. David Owen. The lamps and plants were kindly lent by Mrs. R. H. Pearson. Tea disposed of, the room was cleared for the after meeting, at which the chair was taken by the Rev. J. Yonge:(pastor). The initiatory hymn having:been sung' Mr. YONGH said he supposed he need hardly tell them that it gave him considerable pleasure to be spared to meet there again,his friends and members of the church on that annual occasion. He thought that would hethe 19th annual tea party of Wycliffe Church at which he had had the honour to be present. He was getting old amongst them, but if they liked him as well as lie liked them he hoped they would have some years yet together. (Hear, hear and applause.) The rev. gentleman was sorry that there were some friends, whom he should have liked to have seen, absent from the meeting. I There was Mr. Z. Armitage whose bad state of health prevented his attendance but who regretted very much I that he was not with them and not shle to occupy the position which he (Mr. Yenge) would endeavour to fill. Mr. Wylie was also compelled to be absent owing to a business engagement, and there were other members of the congregation whose hearts were with them but their bodies,unfortunately not. (Laughter.) He had thought, r however, that they might congratulate one another upon the goodly attendance present and that under the cir- cumstancee, all met with a great deal of comfort, as he thought they would all agree with him that the room never looked no pleasing as it did that evening. (Applause.) The committee of friends who so kindly undertook the decorative arrangements, had been very successful in converting the whitewashed . walls into so pleasing an effect. Be was sure th would all be thankful to them and congratulate them upon their success. Speaking of Wycliffe generally, he thought that taking things altogether, and considering the depressing times they had been passing through, they had reason to be thankful for the condition of things existing in the church and ccrigreeation at the present time. With the exception of the inclement Sundays the congregations had been fairly goed,though he was bound to say that upon all opportunities they would be exceedingly enc.: uraged and benefited if their friends would recollect that there was a service on Sun- day morning at 10.30. (Applause.) The evening con- gregations were not to be complained of, hut he had sometimes wished that the members of the Church and congregation would make the effort to be present in the morning. They began New Year's Sunday with cen- siderable improvement, hut the weather since then had been bad, and last Sunday week was exceptionally un- fortunate. It was very pleasing to know that as regards the church there had been a very considerable increase in its membership during the past year, and that was a matter to be note], inasmuch as they had not been in a position to make that statement for several years. The money received by the church during 1894, notwith- standing the depression in trade, still was over £l,OOO. (Loud applause.) If they took into consideration the very critical condition of trade and commerce it was something to be thankful for that £1,033 had been received in Wycliffe Congregational Church. Their P.S.A. meetings still continued to be succes=ful and though they commenced throe sears ago they would see that it still heel the hold upon the men (I the town it had had for the last two years. The Young People's Society still continued .to attract a large number ef . young people on Monday evenings, and the Christian ' Endeavour Society was slowly making its way amongst them. During the last 12 months some of their mem- bers were elected to a position which membsrs of Wycliffe Church never occupied before. They had three members of Wycliffe now upon the body known as ,he Beard of Guardians,—(loud applause),—and the first lady guardian for Warringt)n belonged to Wycliffe Church. (Applause.) There was Mrs. Clay who headed the poll of the town as well as Whitecross ward, mid was fairly entitled t,—and he was sure received—their heartiest congratulations on the fact. (Applause.) Per- sonally, he was glad 'indeed that members of Wycliffe Church had come to occupy positions in the town of such inNence and responsibility. He thought that under no circumstances should such a position be held by persons of one pelitical belief or creed. All Causes should be represented, and he was glad that Wycliffe people were taking their right and proper places in such matters in the town. The rev, gentleman intimated that there were matters in which they wanted more help —in the more iffective carrying out of the Sunday schrol work. He wished them to think it over, and it would be quite possible to double the membership of the school if only they had men and women to take the classes. The speaker wished they would come nut and give themselves to such work for Jesus Christ. There was help wanted at Cockhedge and they must be glad to know that in one ! sense, though they had been without a . pastor there for two years, they were held- ing their own, keeping up the schools and evening congregation. He hoped that it would not be thought that they at Wycliffe were uninterested in their success at Cockhedge. He referred to these few items because it appeared to be desirable to make reference to them. (Hear, hear and applause.) • The musical portion of the programme Was corn- mencarl by the choir singing " Strike the Lyre," in a finished manner which was much appreciated. Mr. W. H. Wallington then sang " Cleansing Fires," in a style both powerful and feeling. Following tins Mr. H. M. Aeliton read extracts from Artemus Wood's " American Humours." The reading was a pronounced success, Mr Ashton's inimitable reading powers being shown to great advantage. provoking roars of laughter from the audience. Miss Booth then rendered l• By the Fountain," with skill and taste, and the Chairman then asked Miss Bessie Dutton for a recitation. tie was sure they would all appreciate Miss Dutton's kindness in coming to render these services and they would all he exceedingly glad to hear her that ev. Miss Berton recited "The child's Dream of a Star" (Dickens), delivering it in impressive and touching voice,the merits of the piece not being Iris upon the audience who loudly applauded. "Our Jack' was a masterly rendition by -Mr. A. Griffiths, who WAS compelled to respond to an enthusiastic encore. Mr. Smith gave" Jolly Jenkin," on the tromhone,highly delighting the audience with his spirited performance. Re hiAO responded to an encore. Another glee entitled "Go Lovely Rose,"was thee given by the choir with the same measure of success. Miss Dutton then treated her highly appreciative audience to a change of style by an amusing fragment from " The Rivals," Miss Booth see, •• Tha Masquerade," and Mr. W. H. Wallington sang the popular song " To morrow will be Faday." Mr. B. M. Ashton followed with" The Chality Hit 12E'r by Litchfield Mosely,the absurd and humorous natu e of which caused a considerable amount of amusement in the audience. Miss Dutton, being compelled to leave at this point, a very hearty vote of thanks for her capital recitations was accorded her. Another song by Mr. A. Griffiths completed the pi-ogre mine. Mr. F. Ft linanwrs then proposed a vote of thanks to Gm ladies, and said it was due not only to them but to the committee and singers. 11, thought that they could no be more indebted to those who had taken the rea- p us hi tel the priwiestines that evening, and he be- ieyed that that wasthe most successful tea meeting they had ever ba,l. (IpM.iii,e.)—Mr.Cienrge Brown seconded the vole, and eke voneurrod with Mr. Roberts about the success of the meeting, they were indebted,too,to those who h w.,rlsed So assiduously to make the rrmm so a'trective, and he honed they would render their set.- v'c s another lime.—The Rev. ELEPHANT HUNTING IN UGANDA.

The ways and manners of uncivilised people are al- ways interesting to their more favoured brethren Their methods ot hunting are frequently specimens f unique skill, though often terriuly barbarous. The natives of Central Africa have a way of killing wild , beasts which is decidedly ingenious. A huge, heavy and p Anted stak ill fixed to a tree,.he ughs fwh cb overnang a frequented track. %Yuen an unsu sprctiug beast is just underneath the murderous weapon, the huge stake by some means is released from the branch and shoots down upon the shoulders or other vital part of the doomed animal, the sharpened point penetrating to a very considerable depth. Instant death, one might imagine, would be the effect of this deep wound. But other methods, less merciful, are in vogue in Africa. Referring to the manner of hunting the elephant in Uganda, a " 'limes " correspondent writes The Waganda, armed for the most part with a long muzzle-loading musket, hunt together in small parties of from eight to ten in number, their method being to get as close to the elephant as possible, and at a given , signal to fire a volley into one of its legs. If the attack is successful, the leg is broken, and the animal instantly disabled, when they finish it off at leisure. More fre- quently the unfortunate beast escapes with a number of terribly lacerated wounds, caused by the iron bolts of from an inch or an inch and a half in length, which are used by the hunters in preference to leaden bullets. In this case the wounded beast retires to the densest and most impenetrable jungles ; where, if not tracked and killed, he undergoes the most protracted sufferings. It is OOP of the most cruel and barbarous methods of hunt- ing, conceivable. Those who are not armed with guns attack the elephant with a short heavy spear from a tree overhanging one wit the regsdar elephant tracks thy nigh the forest. As the unsuspecting animal passes beneAth, they drive the spear down with terrific force, aiming at either the nape of the neck or the spine. If the spear is well delivered, the animal drops instantly ; but, should the hunter miss the vital spot, the beast rushes away with the heavy spear in his body. The weighty shaft stets as a lever upon the keen-edgod head in addition to the blows it receives from the various ob- structions through which the animal forces its pass lee. , In this way a most frightful wound is produced, which causes the elephant to siuk eventually from loss of ' blood.

THE 11A,TEit BAKERS' ASSOCIATION.—SE MR JOHN TOFT'S PRO7sECUTION. Sir,—lt has come to our ears that the late legal pro- ceedings against Mr. John Toft, of Bridge-street, for. selling unweighed bread were brought about at the instigation of the above association, of which Mr. Toft is not a member. Kindly permit me to say that neither directly nor indirectly have they had anything to do with the action of the authorities in this prosecution. Although our Association has long been aware that short-weight bread has been palmed on the public at a lower rate than the ordinary price by low class bakers, we are sorry to find the same thing done by any respect- able tradesman. There can be no question whatever that all bread must be sold by weight, and if the public, who have the law on their side,woulol only insist on their bread being weighed at the time of purchase, there would be no dishonest bakers. SAMUEL DAVIES. Secretary, Master Bakers' Association. 15 and 17, Wellington-st., Warrington, January 25,1895. WARRINGTON DEBATING SOCIETY AND THE I.L.P. Sir,—l was somewhat surprised to read the letter in your last issue signeA by the secretary of the I.L.Peers (query Peers) and presumably coming from the execu- tive of the branch of that party in Warrington. I have too much respect for any party, independent or other- wise, to offer an insult by word or deed, but I consider Mr. Podmore's unjust attack more a personal matter, though coming in the name of a party. He was present at the meeting, and had the privilege to reply at the time, and will also have the privilege at our next meet- ing. I think he gives his case away when he states that the members of the I.L.P. were led on by their s9ciallat friends to interrupt in such an unseemly manner, and for which they were lectured by each of their own speakers at that meeting. I have no wish to eater into controversy with the 1.L.P., because as I said in my speech, I am in favour of each worker getting his honest share of the wealth produced by his labour, which principle I endsavaur to carry out to those who are in my einploy.—Yours !cc., JAMES HORTON. P.S.—lf the I.L.P. will take my advice they will not allow these effusions from Mr. Podmore to appear so often, because it makes it appear too much like a family party rather thaa an independent party. PASTEURISM, AND THE NEW MEDICAL DOGMA. Sir,—While agreeing, in the main with Mr. F. Summers in his dislike to Pasteurism, its means and its methods, I most sincerely deplore such ill-advised ad- vocacy. Feeling somewhat strooghly upon this question I cannot but view such an attempt at its defence—by such absurd arguments—as more damaging to a good cause than downright hostility. I have no wish to • intervene between Mr. Kenyon and Mr. Summers, but must plead against the latter mutilating our common cause by futile argument and injudicious and mistaken statements. Had Mr. Summers wished to make the Humanitarian cause ridiculous his letter would have been admirable. It is deplorable to see so noble a cause given away to the inadequate advocacy of one who is so badly equipped for the task, who, by exposing him- self so openly to ridicule from the other side—his method the pre-Baconian one of imagining a set of " facts "and then arguing for or against them. He rhapsodises about his " love for nature." Love is blind. This may account for the little knowledge our frienci sof his mistress; and homoeopathy seems to have no 'tence for him. Nevertheless, in his main contention Mr. Summers is right. Pastuerism is a dirty busineas, is dirty morally and physically ; and is part of the prevalent medical " craze " for " immunity." To be " immune" we are told to get our blood poisoned to such an extent—from the poisoned blood of other animals that disease will , not enter therein. Personally I much prefer the old fashioned preventitives of Got-given sunshine, fresh air and exercise and a conscience void of accusation of any complicity in the animal torture involved in the prodaction of this infectious " hell-broth,"—Truly yours. JOHN PEERS. SOCIALISM THE ONLY ROPE OF THE WORKERS." Sir,--Of late we have been treated to a great deal of tontioversy between Messrs. Frederick Monks and Thos. Malkin on the most important of English industries, the Iron trade. Mr. Fred Monks is illogical. Belonging as be does to the commercial individualistic school he ad- vocates competition a out rance to the bitter end. When he returned from his tour round the world, he said that in America and in other countries abroad, trade was rather slack, but if men would give way to the em- ployers and consent to have their wages reduced they Would be better employed. So they would for a short time. But when Mr. Monks also tells the men in War- rington that if they would also consent to have their wages reduced trade would revive in England, it means that if we get the trade of other nations throwth under- working them in wages, and they perceiving that we are doing sc, embarked in the same policy, the result must mean ruin all round in a given time. Mr. Monks was brought up to believe that England was called upon to be the workshop of the world, and the only proper and sensible aim of an Englishman was to go on opening out new branches of trade and commerce. But England has not now the monopoly of the world's markets. Ger- many, Belgium, France, Switzerland, America India, are all formidable rivals, and know the use of machinery as well as England does. Since the discovery of 'the use of steam, English men, women and children have been the slaves of capitalists and supplied the world with the most of its wares. The agricultural population has been withdrawn from the fields and put into the forges and factories to make up all sorts of commodities fer use and for exchange. Of late years foreigners have run England a hard race for the trade of the world and now what do we see? Machinery has displaced human labour to an enormous extent. This year's machinery turns last year's machin- ery into so much scrap iron by beating it in labour- saving and the workers are thrust aside. By the excess cf their greed the capitalists have wrought their own ruin. The economic law is as Immutable as the law of the Universe. If we could manufacture customers along with manufacturing goods all would be well for the capitalist grab-all, but that is impossible. The workers when employed are consumers and users,of use and exchange values. They give their labour for an equivalent exchange in commodities pro- duced by some other workers and by mutual exchanges keep commerce and enteroriso on the move. But now that greedy capitalists have monopolised the machinery and trade for their own benefit and to the exclusion of the workers a great number of the wealth-pro- ducers of the world are out of work,and having nothing now to tender in exchange, the machinery will have to be idle for the want of customers to purchase its produce and capital,labour,machinery and all will be at a stand- still. There is an end to all thicgs, and the capitalists and landlords have had their day. But the time is coining ; indeed it has arrived when there must be a change. No, omnrades, there is no remedy at all in your sugges- tions—your ideas of trades unionism are as obsolete as Mr. Monks' commercial individualistic ideas. Of all the " isms " now in vogue, Collectivism and Socialism are the only two " jams" upon which you can place any reliance. There is no use in tinkering with the State machine; we must have a new one. There are thousands, hundreds of thousands, of men now out of employment in England, and there are many more thousands of acres of good land out of cultivation. Let England resume her old position of independence. Let the soil of England feed the English people. There is room enough for all, work enough for all, food enough for all who are willing to work. Those who do not work when they could let them not eat. The only way

NOTES ON GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

FOOTBALL.

Saturday was again a blank as far as Rugby football was concerned in Warrington. The fixture with Roch- dale Hornets was a league fixture,and will consequently have to be played off. It is a little singular that in a fortnight Warrington should have to cancel their fixtures with the two leading Rochthle clubs owing to the frost. The year 1895 has indeed been a very dis- astrous one thus far, as only one " A " tea' i fixture, and not a single first team match, has been played off at the Wilderspool-road enclosure. It is extremely im- probable also that the fixture with Manchester will be iulfilled. It is a long time indeed since there were so many Rugby matches cancelled during a single month, as there have been during the month which has just passed.

The Association game can be played in much more severe weather than Rugby, and Warrington Associa- tion were enabled to have their revenge upon St. Elphin's for that crushing defeat which they got in the season. The match was only won by one val to nil, so that there was not much between the teams. z•-. Elphin's were unfortunate in having to try to get pa:, Webster, who always refused to let them, and kept out some stinging shots.

Warrington Rugby team are " down " to meet Man- chester at home to-day,and if the frost will kindly leave these parts, the following team will do duty for War- rington i—Boscow„ full back ; Barber, Beesom, Moores, and Burton, three-quarters ; Bate and Foden, halt backs; Turner, Jolley, Dakin, Taylor, Berry, Bailey, Barrett, and Houghton, forwards.

I am glad to see that the Committee who have charge of the Lancashire club competition have seen their way to increase the number of clubs in the first division to twelve. Wigan, Salford and Leigh will be reinstated, and Barrow and Widnes, who are almost certain to head the second division will be " promoted " without play- ing the obnoxious test match. This will add greatly to the interest in the competition, and there is no doubt whatever but that the twelve best clubs in Lancashire will be included. Barrow and Widnes are much too good for the seconsl division, and they have left the ;other clubs behind completely. They will, therefore, find their proper place among the seniors.

The formation of a Northern Rugby Union, composed of the leading clubs of Lancashire and Yorkshire pro- mises soon to be an accomplished fact. A meeting was held in Huddersfield on Wednesday evening, when Mr. J. E. Warren, was the represeutativo of Warrington, and it is definitely decided that the champion club of each county will play at the end of the present season for the proud position of champion club of the north. It has not as yet been decided to form a northern league, but no doubt I,this will come in due course.

Tyldesley's lead over Oldham was reduced by one on Saturday, for they could only make a pointless draw of Swintoa. Oldham had protected their ground in view of their match with Broughton Rangers,but the Rangers refused to play anything but a "friendly," and conse- quently the match did not count. had a good time of it, however, for they actually wo.. by 31 points to nil.

In the Football Association League, there were again some surprises on Saturday. Everton entertained Sheffield United at home and were pretty confident of getting two points out of them. They found, however to their dismay that they were extromely lucky to draw for the United were leading by a goal until five minutes from the finish. This has greatly strengthened Sunder- lands chance for the championship, for they won at Stoke very easily, whereas they lost last year. 'They are sow a point to the good of Everton,aml considering that the latter have to go to the Sunderland and Ashton Villa grounds, Everton'a chances of being champions of the league are slowly passing away. Sunderland will most probably be the League champions of the present sea- son.

At the bottom of the League there is a rare struggle. Derby County seem to be fairly stuck there, and on Sat- urday permitted Wolverhampton Wanderers to win their first match away from home. Liverpool are again in the last three, and there they will certainly remain until March, as they have no leave fixture tinil that month. Stoke are the furthest behind, but has they have a number of home fixtures, they have a slight chance of exceping the test matches. Taking the League all round, there probably never was more uncertainly as to the leaders and " wooden spoonists " as there is this season, and we shall .probably not be able definitely to name them until the last week or two of the season.

A PLAGUE OF MICE.

A Moscow correspondent telegraphe that the enormous damage done in the Government of Kherson by hell mice, which multiplied during the past summer to an unprecedented extent, has led to attempts to extirpate the pest by inoculating som, of them with baeilli which are considered fatal to the little marauders. The mice swarm in the granaries and corn depots, and penetrate even into houses, where they devour everything eatable and ruin leather articles, boots and harness. It is reported that in many plaees the pests have even injured the springing crop of winter wheat. The inoculation on a small scale has been the cause of death in at least 95 per cent, of the cases examined, and preparations are being made, under the orders of the Ministry of Agricul- tur, to apply this method of destruction thrmaghout the entire government, which is tnre than halt the area or England. Little can be done, however, on a great scale until the spring, when the snow has disappeared.

WARRINGTON AKATEITR. PHOTO- GRAPHIC SOCIETY. ANNUAL MEE rING.

The annual meeting of the Warrington Amateur Photographic Society was held at the cornmittee-roorn of the Museum, on Tuesday evening, when the chair was occupied by Mr. H. N. Houghton, one of the vice- presidents. Probably owing t the inclement state of the weather, there was but a small attendance of mem- bers. After the minutes of the last yearly meeting had been read and confirmed, Mr. 11. Pritchard, the score- tary, read the eighth annual report. He stated that he had great satisfaction in announcing that the past year had been satisfactory from every point of view. Al- though dark clouds had hung around, and to some ex- tent blighted the hopes they had chriAhecl at the corn- tnencement of the year, they had managed to make a fair show. There had been a number of new memoers, eight having joined during the year, and 21 having re- signed, leaving a total still upon the books ot 59. He believed the treasurer had a small balance in hand, which was very satisfactorY, considering the extra advantages the Council had offered and the bad trade. The library had been very much in demand. New books had been added to it by the Council on process work, re-touching,&c. The Council regretted the poor attend- ance at the monthly meetings, in spite of the additional meeting held the Becend week in the month, as well as the last week. Demonstrations had been given and papers read at the monthly meetings by members and others, including one by Mr. Birt Acres on the carbon process, and also by Mr. Brown, the inventor of the zenotype, all to stimulate the members into better things. Rambles were arranged to Doncaster,Llandudno, London, Scarborough, Fara., and Harrogate during the summer months. Their annual exhibition of elides was held on April 10.h,and passed off with its usual smooth- : ness, and showed marked improvement from an artistic standpoint. It was hoped that they would have been able to have had an exhibition of prints in the autumn, and the Council did all in their power to promote this. The response was so poor that it had to be abandoned at the last moment. fie had hoped that at the com- mencement of the year,a lantern section would have been formed, but nothing had as yet been dune in that direc- tion. He had come to the conclusion that nothing would make the society succeed unless more social even- trues were held ; that photography alone would not bring the members to the meetings he was convinced. He threw this out as a suggestion. He took that oppor- tunity of thanking those who had assisted him at the monthly meetings, and also the Council. He had so little time at his disposal th tt he must ask as a special avour that they would not re-elect him to the Council, but to elect someone else. Mr. DALTON, the treasurer, stated that the balance in the Treasurer's hands was £2 103. 50. In consequence of the poor attendance it was thought inadvisable to procee-1 with the election of officers, and this was accordingly adjourned until the next monthly meeting on February 26.

NOTES BY THE WA-Y AT EARLESTOWN. (BY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBTJTO/t).

I suppose, to be in the fashion I should say some- thing abOut the weather, which in all conscience is just now severe enough to satisfy anybody who wishes to enjoy an old fashioned winter. The roads about Were, at the time of writing, are covered with a white carpet of snow, and the ponds are nicely froze,si over, but skating is out of the question, ow- ing to the snow. The roads on the outskirts of the town are in a very bad condition for vehicular traffic, but in the town itself the authorities at once took steps to clear the streets, and render them passable for vehicles. This speaks well for the new District Council, and shows they do not intend to incur any fault-fiuding by the ratepayers if they can help it.

- The second meeting of the Council will be held on Monday, and I hope it will pass over without any • acrimonious Uiscussions. Business is what the Council meats for, and I expect they will settle down quietly, and get on with tlse business of the township, of which there is pl silty to-day. The street making question is an urgent one, and should be in the fore-trout of the business. Then there is the police question, about which I wrote a few. weeks buck. 1 know one part of the township is only partially watched, but what can you expect, when there is an insufficiency of police. This is a matter which will have to be taken up shortly. There are other questions which need immediate attention, and 1 hope no political squabbling will interfere with their free discussion.

Now I must hark on to quite a different topic, a religious one this time. The scene is pitched at Newton, that place whore the inhabitauta are so (liscreet, 80 quiet, pnd so eminentl) respectable. Now at Earlestown we do not pretend to be any better than we are, and although we generally get what we want, yet our requirements are 80 exceed- ingly modest, that when we make a request for come new departure we do not alarm people, nor administer shocks to their propriety. But at Newton, although they do things so quietly, they generally burst forth in places least expected. They have a cricket club at Newton, quite an aristocra- tic affair, to which is tacked on a tennis club, and also a bowling Mut), and although the members ap- pear to be 80 happy, yet when they et together at a general meeting there is usually a little simmer-. ing of opposition. The meeting passed over very quietly last week,but one incident happened which. has rather 'dropped tbe club fn my estimation a few degrees.

A well-known member startled the members, although I fancy some of them mu at have had an inkling of what was coming, by proposing that the club grounds be opened every Sunday afternoon during the summer, to enable the members to play cricket, tennis, or bowls. He remarked that they might as well play cricket as get drunk or go skating. Just so; hut I also understood Sunday was a day of rest, which, I take it means a rest from ordinary every-day practices. If a man can- not get enough enjoyment nut of thn allotted six days, then I pity hint. But they are a biod-fearing set of people at Newton, and they decided not to play cricket On Sunday.
