THE EXAM NER. SATUR SAY, FEBRUARY 2 1895 THE APPALLING DISASTER AT SEA.

RUNCORN LIBERAL CLUB.

ANNUAL MEETING.

On Wednesday evening, the annual tneetir g of the members of the Runcorn Liberal Club was held at the Club, Regent-st, There was a large attendance of members. Mr. Clarke occupied the chair, and amongst those present were : Messrs. C. Holt, T. Dean, G. H. Kirkham, J. Stanway, J. J. Bridgewater, J. Green, W. B. Brimelow, H. Hughes, S. Btiley,S. Walton, J. F. Moulton, T. Reynolds, and A. Riley (sec 1.

(contiaued from page 6.) No news as ',o the safety of any more of the passengersl or crew of the lost mail ste.imer Elbe had been received' up to Thursday night, and it is believed that everybody on board, with the exception of the twenty people lauded at Lowestoft on Wednesday afternoon, has perished. The total number of lives lost is now stated to be 374. A steamer was sent out from Lowestoft on Thursday to search for any signs of eitker the missing boats or wreckage, but a blinding snowstorm and the heavy sea that Was running interfered very much with the search. She steamed out to a distance of forty-five miles, however, but could not find anything. There is no doubt now that the steamer which sank the Elbe was the Crathie, owned by Mr. W. T. Moffatt, of Aberdeen, which was on a voyage from Rotterdam to Aberdeen. She returned to Maasluis on the day of the disaster and her captain stated on Thursday that his vessel had on Wed- nesday morning collided with a large strange steamer. He was not able to distinguish the name of the other ship on account of the darkness, and he was under the impression that after the collision the other vessel pro- ceeded on her voyage. He followed her for some dis- tance, but soon lost sight of her. The Crathie had her bows stove in down to below the water-line. The mate of the Crathie, who was in charge when the coilision happened, said that although it was very dark there was no fog. He was on the quarter-deck when he sud- denly saw three lights ahead. The next instant he per- ceived a vessel, painted a light colour, right across the bows of the Crathie. It was then impossible to avoid a collision. A difference of ten yards in the Crathie's rate of speed would have averted it. Immediately after the collision the other ship showed blue and red lights, and the Crathie returned the same signal, which was under stood to mean that neither vessel was in need of assist lance.

SECRETARY'S REPORT FOIL 1894.

Mr. Riley, secretary, in presenting the annual report for the year 1894, said it was pleasingto record that the anticipations ,if continued progress whioh closed the report of 1893 had been justified. The year began with a debit balance at the bank of £460 9s. 6d., and after paying all the working expenses oh the year this amount had been reduced by no less a sum than £99 Is., the de- bit balance on the 31st December, 1894, standing at £361 83. 61. This would have been still further reduced but for the fact that additional accommoda- tion by the enlargement of the refreshment room and lavatory had been provided for the comfort and convenience of the members, and the cost paid for out of the year's income. The refreshment ds- partment had more than paid its way and yielded a considerable margin of profit. 'rhe receipts from the billiard tables and handicaps had been £225 ss. 10d., subscriptions, refreshments, rents, and sale of papers had produced £66 lie. To., an income. of £291 178.5 i. The payments included rates and taxes, gas, coal and heating, £44 93. 9d. ; caretaker and secretary's salary, £5B 103. ; alterations and legal expenses, £2l Ils. 2d. ; refreshments, £l7 6s. 2d. ; newspapers, printing. staticnery, and sundries, £2O 16.4. 11d. ; billiard re- quisites, £l4 Is. ; and batik interest and commis- sion £l6 03. 9d. ; a total expenditure oh £192 16s. leaving as before stated a balance of £99 is., which had reduced the balance on the property account by that amount. It was also pleasing to state that the mem- bership had increased. During the year there had been three political lectures given each being followed by an interesting discussion. The first by Mr.li.Lawton,secre- tary of the Liverpool branch of the Peace Society, on .6 War and its expenditure" the second by Mr. W. S. Callie, secretary of the Financial Reform Association, on " The French Revolution and what it cost us ;" and the third by the veteran W. Fl. Chadwick, one of the lecturers of the National Reform Union, on " Common lands and cominon rights." It would be a great Advan- tage if this side of the club's work was prosecuted with greater vigour, and discussions arranged on some of the many questions which were forcing themselves to the front and which would demand attention at the next general election. To do this the committee appealed to those many Liberals will by their experience and past work could successfully carry on this work and train up the younger generation in the faith of their fathers.—The Chairman, in mov- ing the adoption of the report said that on the whole they had to congratulate themselves upon their present position. He hoped as time went on the edu- cational feature of the club would be attended to with more vigour than had been done before. He thought there was great good to.be got by going into that matter more vigorously. They would, by that means, bring more members to the club and add cheerfulness. It was really one of their chief objects to prosecute party prin- ciples and party objects. (Applause.)—Mr. Dean seconded the adoption of the report, and in doing so said he thought the report and balance sheet was a very creditable one. The actual indebtedness of the c!ub he thought was about £4OO, which was not a large sum when they considered that they were in possession of that building, the billiard tables and furniture.—The Chairman said the present overdraft at the bank was £351. (Applause.)

THE MACK SMOKE NUESA.NCH. LECTURE BY MR. F. MORRIS, F.G.S.

The Liverpool Polytechnic Society held the first meet- ing of the fifty-eighth session on Monday in the Royal Institution. Mr, '. O'Cainnor occupied the chair, and Mr. Themes Morris, F.G.S. of Warrington (president of the society), lectured, selecting for his subject, " Is the preveation of smoke economical." During the reading of the paper, Mr. Morris showed illustrations of 25 to 30 various patents whose inventors claimed that by their use, coal could be economically consumed and smoke prevented. These patenti were all more or less constructed for letting air in at the bridge of the furnace, on the "Split Bridge" and " Argand Bridge " principles. The lecturer demonstrated the fallacy of air being allowed to enter anywhere be- tween the furnace door and the chimney Lop, backing up this by proving that all these ideas had been failures as regards fuel saving and smoke prevention. He said also that human skill has not, is not, nor will it ever be able to dole out the exact equivalent of air necessary fcr the complete combustion of each fresh charge of coals on the furnace grate. Another reason given was, that coals differed so much in the quantity of their consti- tuents as to make the above impossible. The reader quoted Mr. C. Wye-Williams, who had proved "that a cubic foot of air at 60° F., Bar at 30. weighed 536 grains, and that there was not one grain of carbon in a cubic foot of dense black smoke." These data caused Mr. Morris to take notice of the number of tons of coal con- sumed in a whole week from 6 a.m. Monday morning, till 2 p.m. on the following Saturday in 13 puddling furnaces, the gases of which a ent up one of the Bewsey forge chimneys ; also to take notice of the colour of the smoke as it was emitted from the chimney. And although there were some 30,000 cubic feet of smoke gases being emitted every 70 seconds, he was willing to forfeit any reasonable sum' of money to the charities if anyone could prove tha: there was more than one part by weight of unburrit fuel in 2,880 psrts, owing to rarifaction and the smoke on the average being, only a light brown colour. Mr. Morris was severe in his remarks upon the way differ- ent Sanitary Authorities harressed many manufacturers upori4this question, when at the same time the Govern- ment of the country was equally guilty in their works' of the same thing, and quite unable to prevent it. He quoted the case of a large and influential deputation of Manchester manufacturers recently Waiting .upon the Sanitary Authoritiel and imploring them to be. moro lenient in their prosecution in this matter. He also observed that if people only knew what the composition of smoke was manufacturers would not be persecuted , and prosecuted as they now are, and different authorities would exerercise their discretionary powers in a more sensible and lenient manner. After the reading of the paper, a short discussion was commenced, but owing to the lateness of the hour it had to be postponed until the next meeting. Amongst the visitors were some members of the Society of Engineers, who thoroughly appreciated the able paper. The pro- ceedings terminated with the usual vote of thanks.

It Might be mentioned that it was troin this Society, of which he is now pre-sident, that Mr. Morris wou their Premium Gold Medal for a paper read a few years ago.

SHIP CANAL.

-- TEA SEIIPMEN rs TO MANCRESIER DIRECT.

Reference was recently made to the intended ship- ment of a large consignment of tea at Calcutta and Colombo for Manchester direct. The s.s. Linlithgow, belonging to Messrs. Raelourn and Verel, the owners of the steamers which bring about half of the Indian tea crop to London, is now loading at Calcutta for Mao. cheater, Hailing on Friday,and specially calling at Ceylon to complete with further consignments of tea for Man- chester. The Linlithgow is expected to leave Ceylon on the 10th February and arrive in Manchester about a month later. We understand that the rate of freight which the steamship owners have taken tur the convey - ance of the tea to Manchester in the same rate as is paid In London, and this will enable the tea to be sold in Man cheater at the same price as in London. With the co- operation of the tea merchants of the district it is hoped that Manchester will during the next season become a centre for the importati on and distribution of tea, not- withstanding the fact that imports have previously been attempted both at Liverpool and Glasgow and have failed in both cases.

A HEARTLEt•S BIGAMIST.

At Berke winter assizss at Reading, on Thursday, be- fore Mr. Justice Grantham, Douglas Campbell, with many aliases, wee found guilty of larceny and bigamy. It was proved that he had several times gone through the form of mat riage with different females, and had obtained money from them and then decamped. The judge described the case as one of the most heartless character, and sentenced the prisoner to the fully penalty of seven years' penal servitude.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

WYCLIFFE P.S A. There was again a large attendance at the usual Sunday afternoon meeting of the above, which was pre- sided over by Mr. J. Owen. The speaker was Mr. A. Corner, who delivered a highly intellectu 1 address upon " Harps of God." Revelations 15c. 2v. Mr. W. H. Wallington was the soloist, and he sang " Cleansing Fires," with fine effect. The service was also assisted by the presence of Messrs. James Ball and C. H. Mills. BOLD-STREEr WESLEYAN CHURCH. The usual People's Service in connection with the above was held on Sunday afternoon and was con- ducted by the Rev. G. D. Mason, Mr. W. Hickman gave the address. lie said the subject on which he wished to speak was " Happy homes, and how to make them. He was anxious to say a few words both to the parents and children present. There were, he said, certain rules and laws to be observed in order that homes might be what they would all like them to be. There must be, first of all, a head, a wise head, to whom the other members of the family could look up with confidence and satisfac- tion. There must be a firm hand, and there must also be sacrifice and self-denial on the part of the parents for the sake of their children. On the part of both parents and children, there should be kind- ness and love to unite in one holy band the family together. With regard to the duty of the children, there must be obedience,there must be no prodigals and no black sheep in the family. It was, he said, absolutely necessary, that they should highly esteem their parents, and ever keep in mind the force of that truth, " With what measure ye mete, it shall be measured to you again." And !act, but not least, there must be bible-reading and prayer. If they observed these rules, their lives would not fail to be bright and happy, and the home on earth would be a foretaste of the home in heaven. Miss Wheatley effectively sang a solo, and the orchestral band rendered several selections of music.

PETTY SESSIONS.

• WEDNESDAY. —Before Messrs, T. Rigby, C. Reynoldg, A.

Thomas and Dr. iteele.

DRUNK ON LICENSED PRENISES.—WiiIiaM WOITEL of Overton, was charged with being drunk on the licensed premises of the Travellers' Rest, in the occupation of Mary Ellames, on the Ist inst. David Ellames deposed that the defendant visited his mother's public-house on the date named. Efe was intoxicated and refused to leave when requested. The offence was admittad and thA defendant was ordered to pay 10s. including costs. DRIINEHNNESS.—Frederick Didsbury, Main-street, avid Alfred Clays, Main-street, of Frodsham, were each fined Sc. including costs for being drunk and disorderly on the 22nd December and January let respectively. STEALING AN OVERC/bAT.—John Smith, labourer, New- town, Fredsham,was charged with stealing an overcoat, value 103., the property of William Clarke, landlord of the Whalebone Inn, Netherton, on January 29th.—Mary Eliza Clarke, wife of the prosecutor, stated that the prisoner !Fished the house on the previous afternoon about Ave o'clock. He was accompanied by John Wade and remained there about two hours. From what she heard, she charged Smith with taking the overcoat, but he declared his innocence.—John Wade, Newton, gave evid,nce as to seeing accused putting something under his coat whilst in the taproom of the public-house —P.C. Price stated that he received information respecting ths theft, and on examining the roads and fields in the neighbourhood he found a coat in a field, rolled up, and hidden behind a tree, about 150 yards from the public- house. P.C. Breeze apprehended the prisoner who ;n reply to the charge said " I know nothing about it, jam innocent. The _prisoner persisted in his innocence in Court. The magistrates considered the case proved,and fined him 10s. including costs, or in default seven days imprisonment.

CRUELTY TO CHILDREN AT WIDNES. HARDWORKING HUBANDS AND DISSOLUTE WIVES.

On Thursday, at the Widnes Petty Sessions, before I Councillor W. W. Gossage (in the chair), the Mayor I (Alderman S. Sadler), and Mr. J. Beecham, a labourer, named Richard Atkinson, and his wife, Mary Atkinson, living at 70. Terrace-road, were summoned by the National Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Chil- dren for neglecting their five children between the 20th of October and the 21st ult., in such a manner as to cause them unnecessary pain and suffering.—Mr. J. W. Knowles, who prosecuted, said the defendants had six children, but one was over the ago of sixteen, and they had the admission of the woman herself that her eldelt son aged eighteen, gave her 12i. a week out of his earnings, and she also said her husband, who was employed by the Corporation, gave her 20s. a week. He (Mr. Knowles) understood that the male defendant was a hard-working man, and had followed his employ- ment regularly for the last six years, and, although he was legally responsible, morally he was not,and he (Mr. Knowles) asked the Bench not to either line him or send I him to gaol, but let him off with payment of costs.— Inspector M'Ateer said it was the worst case lie had ever dealt with. The house was in a filthy condition. There was very little furniture either upstairs or downstairs. The children—Edith Ellen (14), Peter (12), Nancy (6), Jessie (4), and Joseph (16 months)—were in an inde- scribably filthy condition. They were very scan- tily clothed, and covered with vermin. This evidence was corroborated by Police-sergeant Rad- cliffe and Dr. O'Keeffe.—The male defendant was or- dered to pay the costs (103. 6 I.), and the woman was committed to gaol for two months. James Keating, a labourer, residing at 8, Lee-street, and his wife, Mary Ann, were summoned by the society for neglecting their two children, Bartholomew (aged II) and Catherine, aged seven), between the same dates as in the last case. Mr. J. W. KnoWles,in stating the case, said that as in the previous case there was an elder son who earned 17s. Bd. a week, and the male defendant earned 19s. The male defendant was one of those risen who could not manage his wife, and this woman was:a lazy and drunken creature,who cared nothing about her children, and he asked for a severe penalty in her case.— Inspector M'Ateer described the filthy state of the house and children. From inquiries witness had made, the male defendant was a hard-working man, who gave his wife his earnings, but she spent the money in drink.— Police-sergeant Rsdcliffe said the woman was a disgrace to her sex; she was the most disreputable-looking woman in Widnes.—The Chairman said it was another case of a hard-working man being burdened with a bad wife, and he would be only ordered to pay the costs, but the woman would have to go to gaol for one month with hard labour.

WARRINGTON.

GOOD TEMPLARY AT LATCHFORD

On Monday evening, the annual meeting in co nection with the Bonehill Branch Lodge of the In- dependent Order of Good Templars was held in the Temperance Hall Latchford. An excellent tea was provided,to which full justice was done by the large attendance of friends and members. After tea an interesting programme was rendered, the chair being occupied by Mr. J. Harrison, of Liverpool, formerly stationmaster at Latchford, and late member of the lodge. After a most encouraging address by the Chairman the programme was proceeded rith and thoroughly appreciated. Bro. W. Sandford moved a vote of thanks to the Chair- man, which was seconded by Bro. H. Richardson, supported by Bros. F. A. Jenkins and B. Few, and carried. Mr. J. Harrison suitably responded, and the meeting closed by singing and prayer. DEPARTURE OF THE REV. E. Moons.

Raving been supplying the Baptist -Church at Bootle for 3i years, the people there have extended to the Rev. E. Moore, of Warrington, a very hearty call: to undertake the entire pastorate, and the rev. gentleman has accepted. At a meeting of the Warrington Evangelical Noncon- formist Ministers' Fraternal on Monday evening at Mr. Moore's house, Latchford, he was bade a very cordial farewell, several gentlemen speaking in very kind terms of their high esteem of his services as secretary for the Fraternal during the past 10 years. The Rev. E. T. Reed (Emmanuel Reformed Church) was appointed secretaty in the Rev. E. Moore's place. The congregation of the Baptist Chapel at Bootle are about to build a new church and schools in Stanley-road. Considerable prosperity has been enjoyed there during the past and there is every prospect of a prosperous future. Mr. Moore, though leaving the town next week, intends to re- tain all his educational work here for some time to come.

ST. ANN'S CHURCH CONGREGATIONAL TEA MEETING.

The St. Anne's congregational tea meeting was held on Tuesday evening, when about 170 sat down to an excellent tea in the schoolrooms, Dannett-st. After tea an excellent concert wa4 given. Mr. John Fairclough presided, and was supported on the platform by the Rev. J. Davies and the Rev: P. J. Byrne.—The Chairman said he thought they would all agree with him that Mr. Davies was the right man in the right place, and he hoped they would help him all they could. A little sympathy went a long way. Mr. Davies had done a great work in the parish, and he (Mr. Fairclough) was sure it they gave him the necessary help that parish would be a very different one in a very short time.—Dur- ing an interval in the programme. the Rev. E. J. Davies gave a description of the work done during the past twelve months. He said the church had been renovated at a cost of £2OO. The schools had also had £lOO spent on them, so that they had ex- pended on their church and schools during the past twelve months £3OO. The offertories had been .£95 and some odd shillings.—Miss C. Wells was well received for her rendering of " The Minstrel Boy." Mozart's "Gloria" was given by the choir and band in a way which earned for them loud ap- plause, and Mr. F. Taylor was recalled for his ex- cellent rendering of the song, "The Owl." Mr. Abdy received an encore for his singing of "Come into the garden, Maud," and he responded with "My sweetheart when a boy." The band were very sue- cesstul in their performance of "Il Trovatore," and Mr. A. Grounds "brought down the house" with his comical sketch, "A Juvenile Party." Miss Milne and Mr. Randall were recalled for their auto-harp and mandoline duet, "Fantasia on National Melo- dies," and responded with "Scenes that are brightest," for which they were loudly applauded. Master Fred Randall was loudly cheered for his song in character "The Little Alabama Coon," and repeated the last verse in response. A ventriloquial sketch by Mr. A. Grounds caused great amusement. Mr. E. A. Podmore acted as accompanist, Mr. W. H. Rigby as leader of the band, and Mr. P. Randall as conductor. The proceedings were brought to a close with the usual vote of thanks.

Sr. JAMES' CII-ITE-C-H- iiiIIOI,I7ITL—TRA MEETING. The annual parochial tea meeting in connection with St. James' parish was held on Monday, when there was a very good number present. The follow- ing is a list of those who had either given trays or half trays :—Mesdames James Fairclough, Slater, Hull, C. S. Burgess, George Venn, Hutchinson, Robert Davies, John White, T. E. Gibson, Warbur- ton, Stansfield, 11. N. Houghton, Shaw, Joseph Davies, Herbert Woods, Cook, Greenough, F. Booth, Taylor, J. H. Warburton, J. A. Edelsen, Sharp, John Coxon, T. Jackson, W. Jackson, Edward Woods, Francomb, Howard ; the Misses Dowling, Pierpoint, and Parry, and Messrs. Wynne Jones, James Tinsley, Wright, Milling and Co., "A Churchman," Pierpoint and Co., and the Rev. B. B. Prescott. After tea, a musical entertainment was given, presided over by the Vicar (the Rev. F. Slater.) The opening piece was a brilliantly played pianoforte duet by Messrs. L. Lane and F. Booth, followed a song en'itled " Rage, thou angry storm," by Mr J. H. Warburton. A song in character by Mr. B. Booth, "7th Royal Fusiliers," elicited much applause, assistance in the chorus being rendered by one or two of the younger mem- bers of the Boys' Brigade. In the absence of Mrs. W. E. Atkin, a couple ef songs were very tastefully rendered by Miss Pemberton, namely, "Romance of Life," and " Waiting for the King." She was awarded a hearty encore for her first contribution. The humorous element was well sustained by Mr. W. Litton who was several times warmly encored. Several members of the chair and Mr. J. H. Warbur- ton were heard to effect., the former in the lullably " Sweet and Low" and the lattdr in "To-morrow will be Friday." An admirable rendering was given of the humorous trio " Three Young Maids" by the Misses Pollitt and K. Booth. Mr. F. Booth proved himself a highly efficient accompanist.—At the conclusion of the entertainment dancing was commenced, and was continued with evident en- joyment for about two hours.

"THE VIRTUE OF VACCINATION."

A matter that recently excited popular attention was discussed at the meeting of the Baptist Mutual Improve- mient Society, held in the schoolroom. Galborne-street. on Friday evening. Mr. Henry Walker presided, and there was a fair attendance. Mr. John S. Green intro- duced the subject, " Is there any virtue in vaccination?" and said he took the negative side of the question be- lieving it to be the side of safety and of right. He was convinced that vaccination was founded on fancy, and flourished on fraud. What was vaccine ? It was septic matter from a disease, and how impurity could fortify any person's system lie failed to see. Yet what was this filthy rite to effect ? Nothing short of the ex- terpation of small-pox, and for this pretentious claim Jenner was rewarded by Parliament with 230,000. The money would have been better thrown into the sea, than used to commend a system that had wrought so much mischief to the civilised people of the world. as to how far that claim had been substantiated, attentinn need only to be called to the recent outbreak of small- pox in Warrington and to the fact that the disfase flourished as much now as ever it did. How it was claimed that vaccination afforded " protection " from small-pox, but Mr. Green argued that this was a fallacy, as well as the claim of protection from re-vaccination. It was further claimed for vaccination that, even if it failed to protect from small-pox, it mitigated ..he disease, but the speaker discribed this as an utterly, absurd claim. In fact, the claims of the vaccinists were altogether imaginary, and in proof of this assertion, reference was made to the "shifts" astopted by the Jennerites te bolster up the abominable practice. They firstly assert that a single vaccination would protect for life; secondly, that foLir good marks were necessary ; thirdly, that re-vaccination would act the charm, and now they insisted upon vaccination every seven years. Sorely after such shifts as these, vaccination justified the description given to it by Dr. Creighton—" a grotesque superstition !" Why was vaccination kept up? Simply to put money into the pockets of its practitioners, who, according to medical testimony, had never studied vaccination and who kept it " above reproach" by giv- ing false certificates- of death from this filth. Still, if vaccination had failed in one direction, it had succeeded beyoad all expectation in another. It had succeeded in establishing new diseases of terrible and disgustirg character, which caused 10,000 deaths every year. The deaths from eve of these diseases—syphilis, cancer, wasting disease, blood-poisoning, and skin diseases, exceeded the total deaths from smallpox. Theao dis- eases, too, had steadily increased since vaccination was enforced by penal laws, and the speaker appealed to the audience as to whether they could further support a system that worked b 0 much evil. The most odious feature of vaccination was the compulsion that was ax- ercised, and Mr. Green demanded the right of every man to exercise his own judgement on ibis subject. The vaccination laws Interfered with the liberty of the sub- ject, and by repeated penalties tended to bring the law Into contempt. In conclusion the speaker urged his hearers to think for themselves and to join the ranks of those who were doing their utmost to impede the pro- gress, and to seek the prohibition, of "this devastating Jennerian juggernaut." In the discussion which fol- lowed Messrs. J. A. Heywood, W. Drewett, A Balm- ford, the Chairman and others took part'—On the pro- position of Mr. Nalmford, seconded by the Rev. D. W Roberts, a vote of thanks was accorded to Mr. Green for his address, and a similar compliment having been passed to the Chairman, the proceedings terminated.

ELEMENTARY EDUCATION AT HAYDOCK.

One man's loss is said to he ano'her's gain, but this was not the experience of Mr. James F. Joy. of es,. Corporation-street, Manchester, for he lost 50 pounds and no one benefited • and a good reason why, for it mysteriously and rapidly disappeared ; vanished, as were, into thin air ; and just how and why lv lost it ha cannot explain. But it was restored to him in an equally astonishing manner, and reads more like a romance than an every-day occurrence. " About four years ago I had an attack of influenza, which kept me in bed four weeks. When I got up and about again I it was very nervous ; could get no sleep, could not eat, ~ and suffered with pains in the head and face, which al- I most droveme mad. I got worse and e orse week after week. Of course I had medical advice, and various kinds of medicine, f rem of which. I received any benefit. When things got to the worst, and everybody, including myself, had given up all hope, I met a friend whom I had not seen for some time, and seeing the condition I was in (I had lost 4 stone in weight), he said, • Try Warner's "Safe" Cure.' I laughed at the idea. No patent medicine for me, thank you.' But he insisted, saying : ' Don't got one bottle and Use it, and then say it is no good. Your case is too serious for that. It has , taken years to bring you t) your present condition, and you won't be cured in a week, but if yoa will get bottle after bottle for a month, and take•same as per instruc- tions, and you do not find yourself well on the road to recovery at the end of that time. I will pay for all you have taken, and stand a guinea silk hat into the ' bargain." I yielded and got a large bottle, and telig;.— iously took eight doses a day. When that was done got another, and followed suit. At the end of weeks I felt no better, though everyone eke congratul- : ated ma upon the improvement that had taken Place' ‘, This encouraged met) go on, which I did for about four months. At the end of that time I was as well as ever bad been in ray life, gaining weight all the time. Sin" then I have .ever had a day's illnes."

• NEGRO WORSH IP. The Rv. 11. R. Ilaweia, who left England some time Sgo to escape 8..5]e of the rigour of an English winter, and to dc some temporary preaching at San Francisco, travelled from New York o New Orleans, where he was taken by the rector of St. Paul's to a negro revival meeting. "That service," he says in an interview re- ported in the " San Francisco Chronicle," " was a reve- lation to me. I had never seen anything like it. In some way the minister, an immense black man with mighty lungs, leaned of my presence and insuted on the platform with him. So I joined him,ai.d 1.1: nearly wrung me hand off welcoming me and into . ducir g rso. to the congregation. Then he gave Iris flick an ackful spiritual thrashing. The shouting at times was simply terrific. But the black man seemed ti.rribly in earnest; and the sermon interested me very much."
